# Presidency of Joe Biden

For his vice presidency, see [Vice presidency of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Joe_Biden).

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Joe Biden presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Joe_Biden_presidency).

[Joe Biden](145422.md)'s tenure as the [46th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States) [president of the United States](24113.md) began with [his inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden) on January 20, 2021, and ended on January 20, 2025. Biden, a member of the [Democratic Party](5043544.md), had previously served as the [47th vice president from 2009 to 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Joe_Biden) under President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), took office after defeating the [Republican](32070.md) incumbent president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md). Upon his inauguration, he became the [oldest president in American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_age), breaking the record set by [Ronald Reagan](25433.md).[^cite_note-1] Alongside Biden's presidency, the Democratic Party also held their majorities in the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) and the [Senate](24909346.md) during the [117th U.S. Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/117th_United_States_Congress) following the [2020 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_elections), thereby attained an overall federal [government trifecta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_trifecta#United_States). Biden entered office amid the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md), an [economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States), and increased [political polarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_polarization_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-2]

Day one actions of his presidency included restoring U.S. participation in the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md), revoking the permit for the [Keystone XL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keystone_XL) pipeline and halting funding for the [Mexico–United States border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall).[^cite_note-Everett-2022-3] On his second day, he issued a series of executive orders to reduce the impact of [COVID-19](63030231.md), including invoking the [Defense Production Act of 1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Production_Act_of_1950), and set an early goal of achieving one hundred million [COVID-19 vaccinations](63319438.md) in the United States in his first 100 days.[^cite_note-Klein-2021-4] The first major legislation signed into law by Biden was the [American Rescue Plan Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021), a \$1.9 trillion [stimulus bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulus_\(economics\)) that temporarily established expanded unemployment insurance and sent \$1,400 stimulus checks to most Americans in response to continued economic pressure from COVID-19.[^cite_note-5] He signed the bipartisan [Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act), a ten-year plan brokered by Biden alongside Democrats and Republicans in Congress to invest in American roads, bridges, public transit, ports and broadband access.[^cite_note-6]

Biden proposed a significant expansion of the U.S. social safety net through the [Build Back Better Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Act), but those efforts, along with [voting rights legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_to_Vote_Act), failed in Congress. In August 2022, Biden signed the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022), a domestic appropriations bill that included some of the provisions of the Build Back Better Act after the entire bill failed to pass. It included significant federal investment in climate and domestic clean energy production, tax credits for solar panels, electric cars and other home energy programs as well as a three-year extension of [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) subsidies, an [insulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin_\(medication\)) price cap, and a provision allowing [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) to negotiate drug prices. In late 2022, Biden signed the [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act), which repealed the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) and codified [same-sex](92656.md) and [interracial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage) marriage in the United States. Other domestic legislation signed during his term included the [Bipartisan Safer Communities Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipartisan_Safer_Communities_Act), the first major federal gun control law in nearly three decades;[^cite_note-7] the [CHIPS and Science Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CHIPS_and_Science_Act), bolstering the [semiconductor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor) and manufacturing industry; the [Honoring our PACT Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honoring_our_PACT_Act_of_2022), expanding health care for US veterans; the [Electoral Count Reform and Presidential Transition Improvement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Count_Reform_and_Presidential_Transition_Improvement_Act_of_2022); and the [Juneteenth National Independence Day Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth), making Juneteenth a [federal holiday in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_holidays_in_the_United_States). Biden also unsuccessfully pushed for legislation protecting the right to [abortion](765.md) in response to the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md)'s decision in *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* overturning *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)*.[^cite_note-8]

Biden appointed [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson) to the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md)—the first [Black woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_women) to serve on the court. In response to the [debt-ceiling crisis of 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_States_debt-ceiling_crisis), he negotiated and signed the [Fiscal Responsibility Act of 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_Responsibility_Act_of_2023), which restrains federal spending for fiscal years 2024 and 2025, implements minor changes to [SNAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_Nutrition_Assistance_Program) and [TANF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_Assistance_for_Needy_Families), includes energy permitting reform, claws back some [IRS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Service) funding and unspent money for [COVID-19](63136490.md), and suspended the [debt ceiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_debt_ceiling) to January 1, 2025.[^cite_note-9] He established the [American Climate Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Climate_Corps) and created the first ever [White House Office of Gun Violence Prevention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Office_of_Gun_Violence_Prevention). On September 26, 2023, Biden visited a [United Auto Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Auto_Workers) picket line during the [2023 United Auto Workers strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Auto_Workers_strike), making him the first US president to visit one. Biden also rigorously enforced antitrust laws by appointing [Lina Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lina_Khan) to head the [FTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission).[^cite_note-Khan-10] Biden issued more individual pardons and commutations than any other president,[^cite_note-APpardons-11] including controversial sweeping pardons of members of his family and high-profile political figures that he claimed were either presently or expected to be the subject of "baseless and politically motivated investigations."[^cite_note-cnbc.com-12][^cite_note-bbc.com-13][^cite_note-cnn.com-14] The first sitting U.S. president to oppose the [death penalty](412425.md), Biden commuted the sentences of nearly all inmates on federal death row to [life imprisonment](148681.md) weeks before leaving office.[^cite_note-15]

The foreign policy goal of the Biden administration was to restore the U.S. to a "position of trusted leadership" among global [democracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) in order to address the challenges posed by [Russia](25391.md) and [China](5405.md). Biden signed [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS), an international security alliance together with [Australia](4689264.md) and the [United Kingdom](31717.md). He supported the expansion of [NATO](21133.md) with the additions of [Finland](10577.md) and [Sweden](5058739.md). Biden approved a raid which led to the death of [Abu Ibrahim al-Hashimi al-Qurashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ibrahim_al-Hashimi_al-Qurashi), the leader of the [Islamic State](9087364.md), and approved a drone strike which [killed Ayman Al Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Ayman_al-Zawahiri), leader of Al-Qaeda. He completed the [withdrawal of U.S. military forces from Afghanistan](66883576.md), declaring an end to [nation-building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation-building) efforts and shifting U.S. foreign policy toward strategic competition with China and, to a lesser extent, Russia.[^cite_note-ShearTankersley-16][^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] During the withdrawal, the [Afghan government collapsed and the Taliban seized control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive), leading to Biden receiving bipartisan criticism. He responded to the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) by imposing [sanctions on Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) and providing Ukraine with over \$100 billion in [combined military, economic, and humanitarian aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War).[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] During the [Gaza war](74998519.md), Biden condemned the actions of [Hamas](13913.md) and other Palestinian militants as terrorism, and announced [American military support](65284610.md) for [Israel](9282173.md); he also sent humanitarian aid to the [Gaza Strip](12047.md) and brokered a [four-day temporary pause and hostage exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Gaza_war_ceasefire) in 2023 followed by a [three-phase ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) in January 2025. In 2023, Biden negotiated the [Camp David Principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_David_Principles), a security pact between [Japan](15573.md), [South Korea](27019.md), and the United States. He also oversaw the [2024 Ankara prisoner exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Ankara_prisoner_exchange), the largest [prisoner exchange](17209653.md) since the end of the [Cold War](325329.md), involving the release of 26 individuals, including American journalist [Evan Gershkovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Gershkovich) and former U.S. Marine [Paul Whelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Whelan).

Biden began his term with over 50% approval ratings; these fell significantly after the withdrawal from Afghanistan,[^cite_note-21] and remained low as the country experienced high inflation and rising gas prices, even as they later decreased during his presidency.[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] His [age and mental fitness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden) were a frequent subject of discussion throughout his presidency,[^cite_note-24] ultimately culminating in his decision to [withdraw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) his bid for re-election in the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-25] Trump won the election against Biden's preferred successor, [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris), making him the second U.S. president to be succeeded in office by his predecessor.[^cite_note-26] Biden oversaw the strongest economic recovery of any [G7 nation](1196634.md) post COVID-19 and one of the strongest economic recoveries in United States history, breaking a 70-year record for low unemployment,[^cite_note-27] and the creation of over 16 million new jobs, the most of any single term president.[^cite_note-28] During Biden's time in office, median [wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wage) stagnated,[^cite_note-\:1-29] and the share of [wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_the_United_States) of the wealthiest 0.1% of Americans continued to increase.[^cite_note-\:2-30]

Although some political scientists and historians have [rated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_U.S._Presidents) Biden's presidency favorably,[^cite_note-31] his political legacy is interwoven with the re-election of Trump in 2024.[^cite_note-legacyNeg1-32]

## Milestones

### 2020 election

Main articles: [Joe Biden 2020 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign), [2020 United States presidential election](48410011.md), and [2021 United States Electoral College vote count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count)

Further information: [2020 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_elections), [2020 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), and [2020 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_National_Convention)



2020 Electoral College vote results

Biden, who unsuccessfully sought the Democratic nomination in [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_1988_presidential_campaign) and [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2008_presidential_campaign), and later [served as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Joe_Biden) the 47th [vice president of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_president_of_the_United_States) under President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) from 2009 to 2017, announced [his candidacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign) for the [nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) of the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) in the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md) on April 25, 2019. In June 2020, Biden secured the Democratic nomination. [Biden selected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection) Senator [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) of California as his [running mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_mate), and the two were officially nominated at the [2020 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_National_Convention).

On November 7, four days after the election, Biden was projected to have secured the presidency. Biden won the [presidential election](48410011.md) with 306 electoral votes and 51.3% of the popular vote, while [Donald Trump](4848272.md) received 232 electoral votes and 46.8% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-Epstein-34][^cite_note-Budryk-35][^cite_note-Levin-2020-36][^cite_note-Young-37] The [Trump campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign) launched at least [63 lawsuits against the results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-election_lawsuits_related_to_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election),[^cite_note-38] especially in the battleground states of [Arizona](59561924.md), [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia), [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Michigan), [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Nevada), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Pennsylvania), and [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin), raising unsubstantiated claims of widespread voter fraud that were subsequently dismissed by courts.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] The [electoral votes were certified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count) on January 6–7, 2021. In the concurrent [congressional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_elections), Democrats secured a [government trifecta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_trifecta) after retaining their majority in the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) and winning back control of the [Senate](24909346.md), leaving the partisan balance in the Senate at 50 Democrats and 50 Republicans, with vice president [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris)' [tie-breaking vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tie-breaking_votes_cast_by_the_vice_president_of_the_United_States) giving Democrats control of the chamber.

### Transition period and inauguration

Main articles: [Presidential transition of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Joe_Biden) and [Inauguration of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden)



Biden as President-elect



[U.S. National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._National_Guard) soldiers at the Capitol, January 20, 2021

Though Biden was generally acknowledged as the winner,[^cite_note-Epstein-34][^cite_note-Budryk-35][^cite_note-Levin-2020-36][^cite_note-Young-37] [General Services Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Services_Administration) head [Emily W. Murphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_W._Murphy) initially refused to begin the transition to the president-elect, thereby denying funds and office space to his team.[^cite_note-washingtonpost1-41][^cite_note-42] On November 23, after Michigan certified its results, Murphy issued the letter of ascertainment, granting the Biden transition team access to federal funds and resources for an orderly transition.[^cite_note-43]

Two days after becoming the projected winner of the 2020 election, Biden announced the formation of a task force to advise him on the COVID-19 pandemic during the transition, co-chaired by former [Surgeon General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surgeon_General_of_the_United_States) [Vivek Murthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivek_Murthy), former FDA commissioner [David A. Kessler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_A._Kessler), and Yale University's [Marcella Nunez-Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcella_Nunez-Smith).[^cite_note-44]

On January 5, 2021, the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) won control of the [United States Senate](24909346.md), effective January 20, as a result of electoral victories in [Georgia](48830.md) by [Jon Ossoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Ossoff) in a [runoff election for a six-year term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_United_States_Senate_election_in_Georgia) and [Raphael Warnock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael_Warnock) in a [special runoff election for a two-year term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_United_States_Senate_special_election_in_Georgia).[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46] President-elect Biden had supported and campaigned for both candidates prior to the [runoff elections](25932.md) on January 5.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48]

On January 6, a mob of thousands of Trump supporters [violently stormed the Capitol](66307490.md) in the hope of overturning Biden's election, forcing Congress to evacuate during the [counting of the Electoral College votes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] More than 26,000 National Guard members were deployed to the capital for the inauguration, with thousands remaining into the spring.[^cite_note-51]



[Chief Justice](31739.md) [John Roberts](1928850.md) administers the [presidential oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_President_of_the_United_States) to Biden at [the Capitol](31979.md), January 20, 2021.

On January 20, 2021, [Biden was sworn in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden) by [U.S. Chief Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Chief_Justice) [John Roberts](1928850.md) as the 46th [president of the United States](24113.md), completing the [oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_president_of_the_United_States) at 11\:49 am EST, 11 minutes before the legal start of his term at 12\:00 pm, EST.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53]

#### Inaugural address

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**[Joe Biden's Inaugural Address](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:Joe%20Biden's%20Inaugural%20Address)**

Biden's inaugural speech laid out his vision to unite the nation, prefaced by the various impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, [economic strife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States), [climate change](5042951.md), [political polarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_polarization), and [racial injustice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_inequality_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-54] Biden called for an end to the "uncivil war" of political, demographic, and ideological American cultures through a greater embrace of diversity.[^cite_note-Baker-2021-55] He cited the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), [world wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_war), and [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) as moments in American history where citizens' "better angels" prevailed, saying that the unity, the solution, must again be invoked to rise from the "cascading" crises of the present; this unity, he proclaimed, exists in the "common objects" that define America: "opportunity, liberty, dignity, respect, honor, and ... truth."[^cite_note-Thrush-2021-56][^cite_note-NPR-2021-57] He explicitly decried [white supremacy](33178.md) and [nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)), calling them an "ugly reality" of American life he vows to defeat that clouds the "American ideal" set out in the [U.S. Declaration of Independence](31874.md) — that [all Americans are equal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_men_are_created_equal).[^cite_note-Baker-2021-55][^cite_note-NPR-2021-57][^cite_note-58] Biden pledged that the U.S. would "engage with the world once again", "repair our alliances", and act as a "trusted partner for peace and security".[^cite_note-59] Near the conclusion of his speech, Biden held a moment of silence for those who died in the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-Thrush-2021-56] Quoting the [Gene Scheer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Scheer) composition "American Anthem",[^cite_note-60] he implored Americans to consider their legacy in answering the "call of history" to protect "democracy, hope, truth, and justice", "secure liberty", and make America a "beacon to the world", insisting that generations of their descendants would judge them on their actions.[^cite_note-Thrush-2021-56]

### First 100 days

Main article: [First 100 days of the Biden presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_the_Biden_presidency)

The [first hundred days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_hundred_days_\(United_States\)) of a President's term have been historically significant since the first term of [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) who, in his first 100 days, worked on both legislative and executive actions to combat [the great depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_great_depression).[^cite_note-61] In Biden's first hundred days, he signed [42 executive orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_executive_actions_by_Joe_Biden), more than any of his predecessors since [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman).[^cite_note-62] Many of these executive orders were reversals to [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s policies and to resume where [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)—under whom Biden [served as vice president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Joe_Biden)—left off after his two terms in office. On March 11, he signed the [American Rescue Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan), a \$1.9 trillion bill to help relieve economic strain due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md).[^cite_note-63]

Biden was inaugurated alongside [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris), the first woman, first [African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American), and first [Asian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American) vice president.[^cite_note-64]

On November 11, 2020, Biden selected [Ron Klain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Klain), who served as his vice presidential chief of staff, to serve as his [White House chief of staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_chief_of_staff).[^cite_note-65] Biden chose [Jen Psaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jen_Psaki), deputy [White House press secretary](284101.md) and [U.S. Department of State spokesperson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spokesperson_for_the_United_States_Department_of_State) during the [presidency of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama), as his White House press secretary. Psaki announced, and has held, daily press briefings for White House reporters. On March 25, 2021, Biden held his first solo press conference after 64 days in office,[^cite_note-66] unlike his most recent predecessors (back to [Herbert Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover) in 1929), who all held their first solo press conferences within 33 days of taking office.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68]

On November 17, 2020, Biden announced that he had selected [Mike Donilon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Donilon) as senior advisor and [Steve Ricchetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Ricchetti) as counselor.[^cite_note-69] [Jen O'Malley Dillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jen_O'Malley_Dillon), who had served as campaign manager for Biden's successful presidential campaign, was named as deputy chief of staff.[^cite_note-70]

### Cabinet

Main article: [Cabinet of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Joe_Biden)

See also: [Vice presidency of Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Kamala_Harris)

Biden selected [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) to be secretary of state, [Linda Thomas-Greenfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Thomas-Greenfield) as ambassador to the United Nations, and [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan) as national security advisor.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72]

On November 23, 2020, Biden announced Alejandro Mayorkas to be his choice for [Secretary of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Homeland_Security) and [Avril Haines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avril_Haines) as [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md).[^cite_note-73] Throughout December and January, Biden continued to select cabinet members, including [Marty Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marty_Walsh), the then current mayor of Boston, as his [Secretary of Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Labor).[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]

Biden altered his cabinet structure, elevating the chair of the [Council of Economic Advisers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Economic_Advisers), director of the [Office of Science and Technology Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Science_and_Technology_Policy), and [ambassador to the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Nations) as cabinet-level positions.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78] Biden removed the [director of the Central Intelligence Agency](1777424.md) from his official cabinet at the onset of his presidency, but he restored it to the [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Joe_Biden) in 2023.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

While administering the oath of office to hundreds of White House officials through [video conferencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_conferencing), Biden called for more [civility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civility) in politics, saying: "If you ever work with me and I hear you treat another colleague with disrespect, talk down to someone, I promise you I will fire you on the spot. ... No ifs, ands, or buts."[^cite_note-81]

## Judicial appointments

Further information: [Joe Biden Supreme Court candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_Supreme_Court_candidates) and [List of federal judges appointed by Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Joe_Biden)

Of the [Article III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_III_of_the_United_States_Constitution) judges nominated by Biden, 235 of them were confirmed by the [United States Senate](24909346.md), including: one [associate justice of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associate_justice_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States), 45 judges for the [United States courts of appeals](70228.md), 187 judges for the [United States district courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_district_courts) and two judges for the [United States Court of International Trade](1220886.md). Biden appointed the most [federal judges](1636627.md) during the first two years of any presidency since [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy).[^cite_note-82]

Notably, President Biden declined to select a nominee for a judicial vacancy on the [Third Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Third_Circuit) in his home state of [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware) even though the vacancy was announced eight months before his term ended.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-Bloomberg_L.P-84] Biden's decision not to fill the vacancy in his home state of Delaware on the [Third Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Third_Circuit) and unsuccessful nomination of [Adeel A. Mangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adeel_A._Mangi) to another [Third Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Third_Circuit) vacancy allowed President Trump to fill both seats in 2025 and flip the Third Circuit to a conservative majority.[^cite_note-Bloomberg_L.P-84][^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86]

### United States Supreme Court nominations



Judge [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson) with [President](24113.md) [Joe Biden](145422.md) in 2022

On January 26, 2022, it was reported that Justice [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer) planned to step down at the end of the court's current term, giving Biden his first opportunity to name a justice to the court.[^cite_note-87] On January 27, Biden reiterated his intention to keep his campaign promise to nominate a [Black woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_women).[^cite_note-Joe_Biden_Supreme_Court_candidates_Campaign_promise-88] On February 22, it was reported that Biden had met with his top three contenders, [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson), [J. Michelle Childs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Michelle_Childs) and [Leondra Kruger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leondra_Kruger).[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] On February 25, it was announced that Biden would nominate Judge Jackson.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-Joe_Biden_Supreme_Court_candidates_Rogers-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] On April 7, 2022, Jackson was confirmed by a vote of 53–47.[^cite_note-95] She was then sworn in on June 30, 2022, at noon, when Breyer's retirement went into effect.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99]

## Domestic affairs



President Biden addresses a joint session of Congress, with Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) and House Speaker [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi), April 28, 2021.

### Health care

Biden strongly campaigned for the presidency on the [public option](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_option), a policy that, if enacted into law, would have offered Americans a choice between maintaining their private healthcare insurance or buying into Medicare. The idea was viewed as a compromise between the progressive and moderate flanks of the [Democratic Party](5043544.md). The Biden campaign described the public option as a "plan to protect and build on [ObamaCare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act)".[^cite_note-100]

However, shortly before taking office in January 2021, Biden's team abruptly dropped the proposal, frustrating many online progressives who already viewed the public option healthcare proposal as a failure to fight the status quo.[^cite_note-101]

The Biden administration rescinded work requirements for [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid) recipients.[^cite_note-102] The administration opened a special enrollment period for the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) as well as extending the normal enrollment period, citing the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] The administration provided larger premium subsidies.[^cite_note-105]

In August 2022, President Biden signed into law the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022). The law allocates \$64 billion[^cite_note-106] for a three-year expansion of Affordable Care Act subsidies originally expanded under the [American Rescue Plan Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021) and \$265 billion[^cite_note-107] for prescription drug price reform to lower prices, including providing Medicare the authority to negotiate the prices for certain drugs with pharmaceutical companies.[^cite_note-108]

That same month, Biden signed into law the [Honoring our PACT Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honoring_our_PACT_Act_of_2022), which expands federal health care access, services, and funding for veterans who were exposed to toxic substances during their service, including toxic smoke from [burn pits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burn_pit).[^cite_note-109]

#### Opioid epidemic

Main article: [Opioid epidemic in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic_in_the_United_States)

Drug overdoses killed 106,699 in the United States in 2021.[^cite_note-110] Opioids were involved in 80,411 [overdose deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_overdose) in 2021, up from around 10,000 in 1999.[^cite_note-111]

In June 2023, U.S. federal prosecutors announced criminal indictments of [fentanyl](141915.md) precursor producers in China.[^cite_note-112] In October 2023, [OFAC](231045.md) sanctioned a China-based network of fentanyl manufacturers and distributors.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114] In 2023, the Biden administration announced a crackdown on [Mexican drug cartels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_drug_cartels) smuggling fentanyl into the United States.[^cite_note-115]

[Rahul Gupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahul_Gupta) led White House efforts to combat the [opioid epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic).[^cite_note-116]

#### COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](63136490.md)

Further information: [U.S. federal government response to the COVID-19 pandemic § Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic#Biden_administration), and [Biden administration COVID-19 action plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_administration_COVID-19_action_plan)



President Biden touring a vaccine manufacturing plant

On January 20, 2021, his first day as president, Biden implemented a [federal mask mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100_Days_Masking_Challenge), requiring the use of masks and social distancing in all federal buildings, on federal lands, and by federal employees and contractors.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-Bradner-118][^cite_note-Everett-2022-3] Biden also signed an [executive order](210541.md) that reversed the withdrawal of the U.S. from the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO), making Dr. [Anthony Fauci](1227250.md) the head of the delegation to the WHO.[^cite_note-Bradner-118] On January 21, the administration released a 200-page document titled "National Strategy for the COVID-19 Response and Pandemic Preparedness".[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] That same day, Biden invoked the [Defense Production Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Production_Act_of_1950) to speed up the vaccination process and ensure the availability of glass vials, syringes, and other vaccine supplies at the federal level.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-2021-121][^cite_note-122] In justifying his use of the act, Biden said: "And when I say wartime, people kind of look at me like 'wartime?' Well, as I said last night, 400,000 Americans have died. That's more than have died in all of World War II. 400,000. This is a wartime undertaking."[^cite_note-123] Biden established the [White House COVID-19 Response Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_COVID-19_Response_Team), a [White House Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Office) dedicated to coordinating a unified federal government response.[^cite_note-124]

According to a report by [Reuters](18998750.md), in mid-2021 the Biden administration ended a military-run [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_in_the_United_States) campaign to spread disinformation about the [Sinovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinovac_Biotech) Chinese COVID-19 vaccine which had begun in 2020 during the Trump administration.[^cite_note-Bing-2024-125] The campaign was described as "payback" for [COVID-19 disinformation by China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation_by_China) directed against the U.S.[^cite_note-126] Primarily targeting people in the [Philippines](15994099.md), the campaign used fake social media accounts to spread disinformation, including that the Sinovac vaccine contained pork-derived ingredients and was therefore *[haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram)* under [Islamic law](28840.md).[^cite_note-Bing-2024-125]

On January 21, 2021, Biden signed ten executive orders pertaining to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-127] In order to meet his vaccination goal of a hundred million shots in his first 100 days in office, Biden signed an executive order increasing needed supplies.[^cite_note-Klein-2021-4][^cite_note-128] Biden signed an order on January 21 that directed [FEMA](73797.md) to offer full reimbursements to states for the cost of using their own National Guard personnel and emergency supplies such as [Personal Protective Equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_Protective_Equipment) in schools.[^cite_note-Klein-2021-4][^cite_note-129] On January 24, 2021, Biden reinstated a travel ban imposed by President Trump on [Brazil](3383.md), [United Kingdom](31717.md), [Ireland](147575.md), [South Africa](17416221.md), and 26 other European countries.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] The travel ban prevents non-U.S. citizens living in the prospective countries from entering the U.S.[^cite_note-133] Biden implemented a face mask requirement on nearly all forms of public transportation and inside of transportation hubs; previously, the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) (CDC) had recommended that such a policy be enacted but it was blocked by the Trump administration, under which the CDC issued strong, albeit non-binding recommendations for mask use in these settings.[^cite_note-134]

In mid-March 2021, Biden dismissed a request by the [European Union](9317.md) to export unused COVID-19 vaccines from [AstraZeneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AstraZeneca) out of the U.S. even though the manufacturer endorsed it and vowed to resupply the doses. The rationale for this decision, which contributed to low European vaccination rates, was that the U.S. had to be "over-supplied and over-prepared", according to White House press secretary [Jen Psaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jen_Psaki).[^cite_note-135] Whereas the U.S. exported no vaccines, the European Union exported 77 million doses to the world from December 2020 to March 2021.[^cite_note-136] Eventually, the U.S. reversed course and gave vaccine doses from AstraZeneca to Mexico, Canada, and Japan by the end of March.[^cite_note-137]

On May 6, 2021, the Biden administration announced that it supports waiving patent protections on existing COVID-19 vaccines so that other countries can produce generic variants, after weeks of pressure from the international community.[^cite_note-138] On May 7, French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) called on the U.S. "to put an end to export bans not only on vaccines but on vaccine ingredients, which prevent production."[^cite_note-139]

On May 26, 2021, Biden ordered [U.S. intelligence agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._intelligence_agencies) to increase their [investigations into the origin of the virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investigations_into_the_origin_of_COVID-19), after reports that researchers at the [Wuhan Institute of Virology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan_Institute_of_Virology) became ill a month before the pandemic began.[^cite_note-140]

In July 2021, amid a slowing of [the COVID-19 vaccination rate in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_United_States) and the spread of the [SARS-CoV-2 Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant), Biden said that the U.S. has "a pandemic for those who haven't gotten the vaccination" and that it was therefore "gigantically important" for Americans to be vaccinated, touting the vaccines' effectiveness against hospitalizations and deaths from COVID-19.[^cite_note-141] He also criticized the prevalence of [COVID-19 misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation) on social media, saying it was "killing people".[^cite_note-142]

Despite months of vaccine availability and incentives, by September many Americans continued to [resist vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_hesitancy) amid rising cases in several states, hampering prospects towards [herd immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herd_immunity). On September 9, Biden stated, "We've been patient. But our patience is wearing thin, and your refusal has cost all of us." That day he issued an executive order directing businesses with more than 100 employees to require vaccination of their workers or weekly testing, affecting about 80 million Americans. The order also required the roughly 17 million employees of health facilities receiving federal Medicare or Medicaid to be vaccinated.[^cite_note-143] Many Republicans asserted Biden's order was an unconstitutional overreach of federal authority, and some Republican governors said they would sue to block it.[^cite_note-144]

The Biden administration responded to the global spread of the [SARS-CoV-2 Omicron variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant) in December 2021 by advocating response by the states instead of the federal government.[^cite_note-145] Throughout the surge, the Biden administration has been criticized for a lack of [COVID-19 tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_tests), exacerbating the spread of the Omicron variant. When questioned about the apparent shortage of tests, [Jen Psaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jen_Psaki) replied, "Should we just send one to every American? Then what happens if every American has one test? How much does that cost and what happens after that?",[^cite_note-146] causing backlash.[^cite_note-147] The Biden administration responded by promising an increased supply of at-home tests later in 2022.[^cite_note-148]

In the midst of an all-time high of new COVID-19 cases,[^cite_note-149] the [Centers for Disease Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Disease_Control) revised their guidelines, recommending five days of quarantine rather than ten without requiring a negative COVID-19 test.[^cite_note-150] This move was criticized by health experts who worry that without [rapid testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_rapid_antigen_test), COVID-positive people may unknowingly spread COVID-19 in workplaces under the recommended CDC guidelines. Others criticize the CDC for implementing this change after lobbying by [Delta Air Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Air_Lines), leading to social media backlash against the federal government.[^cite_note-151]

#### Cancer research



President Biden announces the revival of the [Beau Biden Cancer Moonshot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beau_Biden_Cancer_Moonshot), February 2, 2022.

Biden gave a speech at the [John F. Kennedy Presidential Library and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_Presidential_Library_and_Museum) on September 12, 2022, the 60th anniversary of Kennedy's *[We Choose to Go to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_choose_to_go_to_the_Moon)* speech, promoting his administration's revival of the [Beau Biden Cancer Moonshot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beau_Biden_Cancer_Moonshot), including the new [Advanced Research Projects Agency for Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Research_Projects_Agency_for_Health).[^cite_note-152]

### Economy

See also: [Economic policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)

*[The New Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Republic)* praised Biden's economic record in July 2024, highlighting record low unemployment which had led to an increase in wages at the lower end of the wage distribution as workers had more bargaining power. However, overall [real median full-time wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wages) stagnated throughout his time in office.[^cite_note-\:1-29] The wealthiest 0.1% of Americans further expanded their share of [household wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_the_United_States) from 13.2% to 13.8%,[^cite_note-\:2-30] continuing the trend of the previous decades.[^cite_note-153] While inflation was painful, it has returned near its pre-pandemic rate and was similar to peer countries, though the U.S. economy has grown faster than its peers.[^cite_note-Baker-2024-154] The expansion of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act), the [child tax credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_tax_credit_\(United_States\)), \$1400 [stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulus_\(economics\)) checks, and the expansion of [SNAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_Nutrition_Assistance_Program) benefits boosted balance sheets for low and middle-income Americans.[^cite_note-Baker-2024-154] New business formation rose 30% from pre-pandemic levels, and was notably strong among women.[^cite_note-Baker-2024-154] Biden also signed three major pieces of longer-term economic legislation to repair infrastructure like roads, bridges and water pipes, boost [semiconductor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor) investment, and expand green energy.[^cite_note-Baker-2024-154]

In February 2024, the total [federal government debt](322221.md) grew to \$34.4 trillion after having grown by approximately \$1 trillion in both of two separate 100-day periods since the previous June.[^cite_note-155]

#### Build Back Better Plan

Main article: [Build Back Better Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Plan)

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Presidency_of_Joe_Biden) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(January 2025)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

The [Build Back Better Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Plan) was a proposed framework of public investment in social, infrastructural, and environmental programs. It was eventually divided into three parts. The American Rescue Plan was focused on [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) relief, and was passed and signed into law in March 2021 as the [American Rescue Plan Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021). The second part called for investment in infrastructure and addressing [climate change](5042951.md), and was called the American Jobs Plan. Elements of the American Jobs Plan were the basis for the [Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act), signed into law in November 2021. The third part, the American Families Plan, proposed investment in social policies, such as paid [parental leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parental_leave). The American Families Plan was merged with elements of the American Jobs Plan to form the [Build Back Better Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Act), introduced in September 2021. This passed the [House of Representatives](19468510.md), but failed to pass the [Senate](24909346.md). Continued negotiations led to a new proposal, the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022) which included proposals addressing climate change, healthcare, and tax reform proposals while excluding Build Back Better's social safety net proposals. This was passed and signed into law in August 2022.

#### Consumer price reductions

The administration also pursued lower drug prices by allowing Medicare to negotiate the prices it pays and capping the price of [insulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin_\(medication\)).[^cite_note-Baker-2024-154]

In 2024, Biden pushed to limit [junk fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junk_fees) through the FTC, [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission) and [CFPB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_Financial_Protection_Bureau).[^cite_note-Narea-2024-156]

Biden took [antitrust](666256.md) enforcement more seriously than several of his immediate predecessors, as reflected in the work of [Lina Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lina_Khan) at the [FTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission),[^cite_note-Baker-2024-154] a historic court victory [against Google's search monopoly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Google_LLC_\(2020\)), and a [lawsuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Live_Nation_Entertainment) to break-up [Live Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Live_Nation_Entertainment) and [Ticketmaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticketmaster).[^cite_note-Narea-2024-156]

In August 2024, the DOJ sued [RealPage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RealPage) under allegations that the company's software allowed client landlords to algorithmically fix rents at rates higher than what would be expected in a competitive market.[^cite_note-157]

##### Prescription medication

Main article: [Inflation Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act)

The [Inflation Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act), signed into law on August 16, 2022, authorizes the [Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Medicare_&_Medicaid_Services) to directly negotiate a maximum fair price with drug manufacturers for specific expensive drugs under [Medicare Part D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Part_D), slashing out-of-pocket costs for beneficiaries covered by Medicare, whilst reducing government expenditure by upwards of \$100 billion over ten years,[^cite_note-158] and raising revenue from rebates from manufacturers who raise prices faster than the rate of inflation, effectively penalizing [price gouging](879011.md).[^cite_note-159]

The first series of ten drugs to be selected by the CMS for price negotiation were [Januvia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Januvia), which was reduced from a 30-day price of \$527 to \$113, a number of drugs manufactured by [Novo Nordisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo_Nordisk), from \$495 to \$119, [Farxiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farxiga), from \$556 to \$178.50, [Enbrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enbrel), from \$7,106 to \$2,355, [Jardiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jardiance), from \$573 to \$197, [Stelara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stelara), from \$13,836 to \$4,695, [Xarelto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xarelto), from \$517 to \$197, [Eliquis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliquis), from \$521 to \$231, [Entresto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entresto), from \$628 to \$295, and [Imbruvica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imbruvica), from \$14,934 to \$9,319.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] This series first went into effect on January 1, 2026.[^cite_note-kff-factsheet-162]

The CMS announced the second slate of fifteen drugs to be negotiated downwards in price on January 17, 2025.[^cite_note-163] This slate includes [Ozempic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozempic), [Rybelsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rybelsus) and [Wegovy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wegovy), all manufactured by Novo Nordisk, each negotiated down from a 30-day price of \$959 to \$274, [Trelegy Ellipta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trelegy_Ellipta), from \$654 to \$175, [Xtandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xtandi), from \$13,480 to \$7,004, [Pomalyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomalyst), from \$21,744 to \$8,650, [Ofev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ofev), from \$12,622 to \$6,350, [Ibrance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrance), \$15,741 to \$7,871, [Linzess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linzess), from \$539 to \$136, [Calquence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calquence), from \$14,228 to \$8,600, [Austedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austedo) and [Austedo XR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austedo_XR), from \$6,623 to \$4,093, [Breo Ellipta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breo_Ellipta), from \$397 to \$67, [Xifaxan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xifaxan), from \$2,696 to \$1,000, [Vraylar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vraylar), from \$1,376 to \$770, [Tradjenta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tradjenta), from \$688 to \$78, [Janumet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janumet) and [Janumet XR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janumet_XR), from \$526 to \$80, and [Otezla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otezla) and [Otezla XR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otezla_XR?action=edit\&redlink=1), from \$4,722 to \$1,650.[^cite_note-164] These negotiations are scheduled to go into effect on January 1, 2027.[^cite_note-kff-factsheet-162]

In 2026, the CMS announced the next round of price negotiation for fifteen prescription drugs covered by both [Medicare Part B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Part_B) and Medicare Part D. These drugs are [Trulicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trulicity), [Biktarvy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biktarvy), [Orencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orencia), [Cosentyx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosentyx), [Erleada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erleada), [Kisqali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kisqali), [Entyvio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entyvio), [Verzenio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verzenio), [Botox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botox), [Lenvima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenvima), [Xolair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xolair), [Rexulti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rexulti), [Xeljanz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xeljanz) and [Xeljanz XR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xeljanz_XR), [Anoro Ellipta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anoro_Ellipta), and [Cimzia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cimzia).[^cite_note-165]

In total, coverage of the selected prescription drugs account for approximately 36% of all Medicare Part B and Part D annual expenditures, and reducing their prices would permit large reductions in government expenditure, not just cost-of-living reductions for Medicare recipients.[^cite_note-kff-factsheet-162]

The Act also contains provisions to cap [insulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin_\(medication\)) prices for seniors enrolled on Medicare to \$35 per month, and limit out-of-pocket prescription drug costs at \$2,000 annually.[^cite_note-166] The original version of the Act had a provision that penalized drug price increases that exceeded the rate of inflation for privately insured beneficiaries, but was struck from the Act by the [Parliamentarian of the United States Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentarian_of_the_United_States_Senate) due to not being a budget-related provision eligible to be passed via [budget reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_reconciliation).[^cite_note-167]

##### American Rescue Plan Act of 2021

Main article: [American Rescue Plan Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021)



President Biden signs the American Rescue Plan Act of 2021 into law, March 11, 2021.

On January 14, 2021, Biden revealed a \$1.9 trillion [stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulus_\(economics\)) bill, the American Rescue Plan Act of 2021.[^cite_note-Barrow-168] The plan includes \$1 trillion in direct aid, including \$1,400 per-person checks, for working Americans, and would provide for direct housing and nutrition assistance, expanding access to safe and reliable childcare and affordable healthcare, increasing the minimum wage, extending unemployment insurance, and giving families with kids and childless workers an emergency boost this year.[^cite_note-ReliefPDF-169][^cite_note-NLRrelief-170] It would also expand the eligibility of these checks to adult dependents who have been left out of previous rounds of relief.[^cite_note-ReliefPDF-169][^cite_note-NLRrelief-170][^cite_note-Barrow-168] The plan additionally includes \$440 billion in community support, providing \$350 billion of community support to first responders while the rest goes to grants for small businesses and transit agencies; \$400 billion for a national vaccination plan and school reopenings; and \$10 billion for information technology, modernizing federal cybersecurity infrastructure.[^cite_note-Barrow-168][^cite_note-NLRrelief-170] In her first press briefing, press secretary Psaki said the plan was likely to change as negotiations continued, with the provision to increase the minimum wage later being excluded from the relief plan.[^cite_note-171] Biden signed the Plan into law on March 11, 2021, passing through both chambers of Congress with a [party-line vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-line_vote).[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]

The plan invokes the [Defense Production Act of 1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Production_Act_of_1950) to ensure the production of [personal protective equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_protective_equipment), glass vials, syringes, and other supplies exceeds the demand.[^cite_note-ReliefPDF-169] It allows partners of states to create vaccine centers in stadiums, convention centers and pharmacies.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-2021-121] The federal government would identify communities that have been hit hardest by COVID-19, and ensure the vaccine does not reach them at an unfair pace.[^cite_note-NLRrelief-170][^cite_note-ReliefPDF-169][^cite_note-Associated_Press-2021-121] In addition, the plan would launch a national campaign to educate Americans about the vaccine and COVID-19, targeting [misinformation related to the pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic).[^cite_note-Associated_Press-2021-121] Vaccines would also be freely available to all citizens regardless of immigration status.[^cite_note-ReliefPDF-169] In Biden's plan, he would issue a national testing strategy that attempts to mitigate the spread of COVID-19 by increasing laboratory capacity and expanding testing. The plan would also develop new treatments for COVID-19.[^cite_note-ReliefPDF-169][^cite_note-Barrow-168][^cite_note-NLRrelief-170][^cite_note-Associated_Press-2021-121]

##### American Families Plan

Main article: [Build Back Better Plan § American Families Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Plan#American_Families_Plan)

On April 28, during Biden's speech to Congress he unveiled the American Families Plan, a roughly \$1.8 trillion proposal to significantly increase federal spending in areas related to [childcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childcare), [paid leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paid_leave), [pre-kindergarten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-kindergarten), [community college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_college), and [healthcare](27553159.md).[^cite_note-WaPo-174][^cite_note-WaPo-2-175] It is considered to be the third part of Biden's "Build Back Better" agenda (the first being the [American Rescue Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan) and the second being the [American Jobs Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jobs_Plan)).[^cite_note-nyt-176] The bill was effectively merged with climate change and other provisions that didn't make it into the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act, for a total \$3.5 trillion [Build Back Better Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Act). However, the bill did not have Republican support, and Democrats struggled to win the support of Senator [Joe Manchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Manchin) of West Virginia to pass it on a [party-line vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-line_vote) through [budget reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_\(United_States_Congress\)), even as the price was lowered to \$2.2 trillion.[^cite_note-177] After the bill ultimately failed to match his envisioned cost, Manchin publicly rejected it, dooming its passage.[^cite_note-178]

#### Labor

On January 22, 2021, Biden signed an executive order that removed schedule F, overturning a number of Trump's policies that limited the [collective bargaining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_bargaining) power of federal unions.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] Biden's executive order also promotes a \$15 [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage) for federal workers and repeals three of Trump's executive orders which made the employee discipline process stricter and restricted union representatives' access to office space. As well as promoting a \$15 minimum wage, Biden's executive order increases the amount of money going to the families of children who are missing meals because of school closures due to the pandemic by 15%.[^cite_note-181] The repealing of Trump's three executive orders comes as the orders were used to transfer civil servants and career scientists and replace them with employees friendly to the Trump administration.[^cite_note-182]

On inauguration day 2021, Biden fired pro-business [Peter Robb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_B._Robb), then general counsel of the [National Labor Relations Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Labor_Relations_Board),[^cite_note-183] replacing him with pro-union [Jennifer Abruzzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_Abruzzo) in February 2021.[^cite_note-184] Biden's NLRB has pursued action against [Starbucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starbucks)' and [Amazon](90451.md)'s alleged anti-union activities.[^cite_note-185] On August 24, 2023, the NLRB reinstated [Obama-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) policies regarding union elections, speeding up the timeline by removing restrictions such as resolving [litigation](113258.md) before holding an election.[^cite_note-186]

In late 2022, Biden signed a bill forcing an agreement between union workers and rail companies in order to prevent a strike, earning him criticism from progressives and rail workers.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] Afterwards, Biden pressured the rail companies to offer [paid sick leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sick_leave) to workers, which had been a key demand in the original planned strike.[^cite_note-reuters-biden-pressure-190] More than 60% of rail workers had sick leave agreements in June 2023.[^cite_note-reuters-sick-leave-191]

On August 8, 2023, the Biden administration issued new guidelines governing how the [prevailing wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevailing_wage) that are used to set a wage floor under the [Davis-Bacon Act of 1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davis-Bacon_Act_of_1931) are calculated for federally-funded public works projects; under the new rule, the prevailing wage would be equal to the wage paid to at least 30% of workers in an area, or the weighted average of all wages in the area if no single wage rate covers at least 30% of workers, reversing a rule set by the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan) in 1982 that replaced this rule with a 50% rule.[^cite_note-biden-prevail-wage-192] The administration argued that this would have the effect of raising wages for construction workers because, under the Reagan-era rule, since a single wage rate is unlikely to be paid to at least 50% of workers, contractors would default to using the average wage, which could be brought down by privately funded projects that recompense workers with an atypically low pay, while wage rates covering over 30% of workers are more common and correspond to higher [collectively bargained](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_bargaining) rates.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-biden-prevail-wage-192] However, this rule was blocked by [district judge](834368.md) [Sam Cummings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Cummings) on June 24, 2024.[^cite_note-194]

On the [2023 United Auto Workers strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Auto_Workers_strike), Biden repeated union leader [Shawn Fain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawn_Fain)'s motto "record profits, record contracts" and expressed support for the workers in negotiations.[^cite_note-195] Biden assigned two White House officials to aid in negotiation efforts, senior adviser [Gene Sperling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Sperling) and [acting](691917.md) [Labor Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Labor) [Julie Su](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_Su).[^cite_note-196] On September 26, Biden joined striking UAW workers on the [picket line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picketing) in Michigan, becoming the first president to do so.[^cite_note-197]

On April 23, 2024, the Department of Labor issued a rule raising the cap on mandatory [overtime pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overtime_pay) for [salaried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salaried) workers to \$58,600 per year, up from \$35,500 in 2020, which would extend benefits to over four million extra salary workers. It was challenged by business and conservative groups in court, led by Texas Attorney General [Ken Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Paxton),[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] and subsequently overturned later that year,[^cite_note-200] before being rescinded by the [second Trump administration](78298108.md).[^cite_note-201]

Additionally, on the same day, Biden administration officials, including National Economic Advisor [Lael Brainard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lael_Brainard) and Acting Secretary of Labor Julie Su, convened leaders from five major pension fund managers, including the [New York State Common Retirement Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_State_Common_Retirement_Fund) and the [California Public Employees' Retirement System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Public_Employees'_Retirement_System), to announce an initiative buttressing robust labor standards from private equity investments in the portfolios of these capital funds, including promoting unionization and neutrality to union drives, workplace safety standards, and the elimination of the use of [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor) and illegal [child labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_labor).[^cite_note-202]

Biden became the first US president to run for election with a [unionized](17626.md) campaign staff for his [2024 election](59568546.md) run.[^cite_note-203]

The Biden administration helped to resolve the [2024 United States port strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_port_strike).[^cite_note-204]

##### 2022 United States railroad labor dispute

Main article: [2022 United States railroad labor dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_railroad_labor_dispute)

The rise of [precision scheduled railroading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precision_scheduled_railroading) is controversial has led to longer and more irregular working hours, tighter schedules, longer trains, supply chain delays, higher frequency of train [derailments](1093599.md), depreciated safety standards, staffing and budget cuts, increased workloads per employee, and cuts to railroad infrastructure maintenance.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Additionally, [BNSF Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF_Railway) adopted a new attendance policy called "Hi-Viz", which unions alleged penalized taking sick time-off and incentivized coming to work while ill.[^cite_note-208] These policies eliminate any free time which workers have, requiring them to be effectively on-call for weeks at a time.[^cite_note-209]

The embattled management philosophy had been under dispute between a coalition of twelve railroad labor unions representing over 115,000 railroad workers, known as the Coordinated Bargaining Coalition, and the National Carriers Conference Committee, collating six Class I rail carriers bargaining together, [Union Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific), [Norfolk Southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norfolk_Southern), [CSX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSX), [BNSF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF), [Kansas City Southern Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Southern_Railway), and [Canadian National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_National), while [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) and [Canadian Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Pacific) bargained separately,[^cite_note-210] with pay raises, paid sick leave, labor protections and better working conditions on the table.

Negotiations extended for more than two years, wending from 2019 and through early 2022, with very little progress made, and ultimately reached an impasse,[^cite_note-211] with workers in the [Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers and Trainmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brotherhood_of_Locomotive_Engineers_and_Trainmen) voting to authorize a strike later that year,[^cite_note-212] and the [International Association of Sheet Metal, Air, Rail, and Transportation Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Association_of_Sheet_Metal,_Air,_Rail,_and_Transportation_Workers) threatening a strike in turn.[^cite_note-213] However, growing fears that a railroad strike could lead to further supply chain failure amidst already present [supply chain issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021-2023_global_supply_chain_crisis) ahead of the holidays that could deepen and agitate [existing inflationary pressures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021-2023_inflation_surge)[^cite_note-214] exerted immense political pressures upon the president and Congress to intercede in the negotiations, and invoke the [Railway Labor Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_Labor_Act) to preclude a possible strike.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]

In accordance with the Railway Labor Act, President Biden convened a three-person [Presidential Emergency Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Emergency_Board) with Executive Order 14077 on July 20, 2022.[^cite_note-217] The Board issued a report one month later, starting a 30-day cooling off period that prevents any strikes or lockouts, and recommending "annual wage increases of between 4% and 7% through 2024" in addition to retroactive pay increases, one extra paid day off, and five \$1,000 annual bonuses.[^cite_note-218]

However, while ten unions accepted the terms of the deal, some considered it to be insufficient and prepared to strike anyway.[^cite_note-219] Near the end of the cool-off period, Biden and [Secretary of Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Labor) [Marty Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marty_Walsh) engaged in twenty consecutive hours of talks with union leader and railroad representatives in the [Department of Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Labor).[^cite_note-dol-negotiate-220] These negotiations resulted in an updated deal, including a 24% increase in wages from 2020 through 2024, necessitating an automatic average payout of \$11,000 to encompass retroactive wage hikes, a five-year \$5,000 bonus, a cap on monthly health care contributions, more flexible schedules, like unpaid time off for medical appointments and exemptions from attendance policies for hospitalizations and surgical procedures,[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] as well as the ability for unions to negotiate more regular schedules for engineers and conductors in future contracts.[^cite_note-223] However, the deal only provided one day of paid sick leave, to the dismay of union leaders and workers, who desired fifteen days, and as a result strike fears still loomed from holdout unions.

Congressional Democratic leaders wished not to intervene in further negotiations, not even to extend the cool-off period, with House Majority Leader [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi) remarking that "\[they would] rather see negotiations prevail so there’s no need for any actions from Congress,"[^cite_note-dol-negotiate-220] while Senate Democrats and Independent [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders) defeated a Republican-led bill to impose the terms of the Presidential Emergency Board's advice.[^cite_note-224] But with freight railroads refusing to budge on paid sick leave,[^cite_note-225] and pressures from the fallout of a potential strike growing, Biden called for his party to capitulate and agree to instate the deal from September,[^cite_note-226] which they ultimately did, though House Democrats attempted to simultaneously pass an amended version with seven days of paid sick leave, which was blocked by the Senate due to a lack of Republican support.[^cite_note-227] The bill was signed into law on December 2, 2022.[^cite_note-228]

Afterwards, Biden administration officials, including [Pete Buttigieg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Buttigieg), Marty Walsh and Biden himself, continued to lobby railroad companies to accede to unions' demands for paid sick leave.[^cite_note-reuters-biden-pressure-190] Eventually, the major railroads reached similar agreements with various unions to allow workers have a minimum of four days of paid sick leave, and the option to convert three personal days into paid sick leave days, in the months following, mostly resolving the dispute after years of negotiation, though precision scheduled railroading was still the rule of the land.[^cite_note-229] The Railroad Department Director of [International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Brotherhood_of_Electrical_Workers), Alphonse Russo, thanked the Biden administration for its help in the negotiations.[^cite_note-230]

The percentage of workers with access to paid sick leave increased from barely above 0% before the deal, to approximately 60% of the railroad workforce,[^cite_note-reuters-sick-leave-191] and onwards to above 90% by the middle of 2024, though the administration called on railroad companies to ensure that the remaining 10% of uncovered railroad employees were given access to paid sick leave.[^cite_note-231]

#### Antitrust law enforcement

Main article: [Federal Trade Commission § Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission#Biden_administration)

Federal agencies under the Biden administration enforced antitrust laws more vigorously than seen in previous administrations ever since the [Chicago school of economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_school_of_economics) supplanted the structuralist thought of the early 20th century.[^cite_note-232] This shift was embodied by the administration's embrace of the [New Brandeis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Brandeis_movement) school of antitrust enforcement, represented by Biden's appointment of [Lina Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lina_Khan) as the [Chair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chair_of_the_Federal_Trade_Commission) of the [Federal Trade Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission),[^cite_note-233] [Jonathan Kanter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Kanter) to serve as the [Assistant Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Assistant_Attorney_General) for the [United States Department of Justice Antitrust Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice_Antitrust_Division) of the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md),[^cite_note-234] and [Tim Wu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Wu) to serve as the special assistant to the president for technology and competition policy on the President's [National Economic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Economic_Council_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-235] among others.

Biden signed [Executive Order 14036](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_14036) on July 9, 2021, directing federal agencies to cooperatively engage a seventy-two point "whole-of-government" plan impounding aggressive enforcement of antitrust legislation and jurisprudence to defeat domestic market consolidation and the creation of monopolies or [cartels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartel) across multiple industries, which he believes will foster competition and innovation, decrease costs, combat exploitative pricing, increase consumer flexibility and buying power, and allow workers to better negotiate for fairer wages or working conditions, or seek higher-paying jobs in the event of impasse.
