The [executive order](210541.md) accordingly created a White House Competition Council, which would "coordinate, promote, and advance Federal Government efforts to address overconcentration, monopolization, and unfair competition in or directly affecting the American economy."[^cite_note-236]

The Department of Justice and Federal Trade Commission adopted a more hardline stance on large mergers,[^cite_note-237] reflected in the updated agency guidelines issued in late 2023.[^cite_note-238] The FTC took action against at least thirty-eight mergers between June 2021 and November 2023, with the FTC authorizing staff to file an administrative complaint or seek a [preliminary injunction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preliminary_injunction) in federal court to halt the progress of these mergers. In half of these mergers, the parties involved abandoned their plans after the FTC issued its complaint. In one, the parties structurally modified the terms of the merger and sold off numerous assets in order to comply with existing antitrust legislation in response to the threat of litigation. In another, the parties agreed to a consent order prohibiting them from engaging in the conduct alleged by the FTC. Another fourteen mergers beyond these were abandoned during the FTC's investigation, and with another fourteen, the FTC ordered a series of divestitures to prevent the mergers from resulting in harm to the markets.[^cite_note-239]

The Federal Trade Commission brought to trial four times as many billion-dollar merger challenges between 2021 and late 2024 than it did in the period between 2017 and 2021, and 2008 and 2017. A significantly larger proportion of merger challenges between 2021 and late 2024 ended in the merge attempt being abandoned compared to the 2017-21 interval and 2016 by a factor of nearly three, exceeding the total number of abandoned mergers of the preceding decade.[^cite_note-240]

#### Banking

See also: [2023 banking crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_banking_crisis)

After the [Collapse of Silicon Valley Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_Silicon_Valley_Bank) in 2023, Biden expressed opposition to a [bailout](1469849.md) that was borne by taxpayers.[^cite_note-241] He also claimed that the [partial roll-back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Growth,_Regulatory_Relief,_and_Consumer_Protection_Act) of [Dodd-Frank regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodd%E2%80%93Frank_Wall_Street_Reform_and_Consumer_Protection_Act) contributed to the bank failures.[^cite_note-242]

#### Domestic manufacturing

Biden signed an executive order intended to support domestic manufacturers by increasing a federal preference for purchasing goods made wholly or partly in the U.S. Using the broad term "Made in America laws", the executive order's stated goal is to strengthen "all statutes, regulations, rules, and Executive Orders relating to Federal financial assistance awards or Federal procurement, including those that refer to 'Buy America' or '[Buy American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buy_American).'"[^cite_note-whit_Executive_Order_on_Ensuring-243][^cite_note-cnn._Biden_signs-244]

On August 9, 2022, Biden actively promoted and signed into law the [CHIPS and Science Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CHIPS_and_Science_Act), which authorized \$52 billion for domestic semiconductor research and manufacturing.[^cite_note-245] On August 16, 2022, Biden signed the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022) into law, which included provisions to support the domestic production of solar panels, wind turbines, and other infrastructure.[^cite_note-246] Due to incentives from the CHIPS and Science Act, [Micron Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micron_Technology) will invest billions in new semiconductor manufacturing in [New York](8210131.md).[^cite_note-247]

#### Trade

In 2021 *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported that instead of negotiating access to Chinese markets for large American financial-service firms and pharmaceutical companies, the Biden administration might focus on trade policies that boost exports or domestic jobs. [U.S. Trade Representative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Trade_Representative) [Katherine Tai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katherine_Tai) said the administration wants a "worker-centered trade policy".[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] [U.S. Secretary of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Secretary_of_Commerce) [Gina Raimondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gina_Raimondo) aggressively enforced trade rules to combat unfair practices by China.[^cite_note-250]

In March 2021, in her first interview since taking office, U.S. Trade Representative Tai told *The Wall Street Journal* the U.S. would not lift tariffs on Chinese imports (implemented under the Trump administration as urged by [Peter Navarro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Navarro)) in the near future, despite lobbying efforts from "free traders" including former [U.S. Secretary of Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Secretary_of_Treasury) [Hank Paulson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hank_Paulson) and the [Business Roundtable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Roundtable), a big-business group in the U.S., that pressed for tariff repeal.[^cite_note-251]

In 2021, the U.S. suspended its diplomatic trade engagement with [Myanmar](19457.md) after a rise in violence perpetrated by the [Burmese military](20394.md) against anti-[coup](66567997.md) protesters.[^cite_note-252]

In May 2024, the Biden administration raised [tariffs](55551.md) on Chinese imports, including a doubling for [solar cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_cell); tripling for [lithium-ion electric vehicle batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium-ion_battery); and increases for steel, aluminum, and medical equipment. This marks the first time that critical minerals, including [rare earth magnets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare-earth_magnet)—key components in electric and hybrid vehicles—have been specifically included in the tariffs.[^cite_note-253] China produces and refines over 90 percent of the world's [rare earth material](145440.md).[^cite_note-254] The tariff increases will be phased in over a period of three years.[^cite_note-255]

#### Infrastructure

See also: [Build Back Better Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Plan) and [Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act)



President Biden signs the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act into law, November 15, 2021.

As a part of the Build Back Better Plan, the Biden administration aimed for massive spending on the nation's infrastructure on the order of \$2 trillion.[^cite_note-256] Several of the physical infrastructure provisions featured in the proposal were included in the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act. Biden signed the Act into law on November 15, 2021.[^cite_note-257]

This final version included approximately \$1.2 trillion in spending, with \$550 billion in spending authorized for "new" infrastrucutre projects on top of \$660 billion in funds appropriated by Congress for work on existing infrastructure through reauthorizations and expansions of older federal-aid programs.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-npr20211106-259] The bill included \$7.5 billion for electric vehicle charging.[^cite_note-Osaka-2024-260] As of March 2024, seven charging stations with a total of 38 spots for charging vehicles had been built.[^cite_note-Osaka-2024-260]

#### Inflation

See also: [2021–2023 inflation surge](69605317.md)

Longer-term variables that preceded the pandemic and contributed to the rise in inflation include structural [housing shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_shortage),[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] impacts of climate change on food, energy and home insurance prices,[^cite_note-Becker-2024-264][^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] as well as the size of [government debt](695460.md) and deficits.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

In the midst of recovery from COVID-19, inflation rose to the highest rate in forty years peaking at 9.1% in 2022, with many other major global economies reaching similar level.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] Biden stated during his first [State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_State_of_the_Union_Address) on March 1, 2022, that addressing inflation was his "top priority", while touting an anti-inflation plan that he said would address ocean shipping costs and prescription drug prices.[^cite_note-275]

The [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) resulted in increases in food and energy prices.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277]

At the end of 2023, the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) found that without housing inflation, inflation would have been just 1.8%, instead of 3.2%.[^cite_note-Boak-2024-278] Katy O'Donnell of *Politico* argued that housing shortages, caused by artificial scarcity driven by [NIMBYism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIMBY), had been the single-biggest contributor to inflation.[^cite_note-279] [Freddie Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freddie_Mac) estimated that the housing shortage surged 52% between 2018 and 2020.[^cite_note-Boak-2024-278] Between 2020 and 2023 [climate-change exacerbated home insurance premiums in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_and_insurance_in_the_United_States) by 33%.[^cite_note-Becker-2024-264] July 2024 data showed that inflation had dropped to 2.9%, with rising rents and childcare costs as the main drivers.[^cite_note-280]

Most economists surveyed by the *[WSJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WSJ)* in July 2024 found that inflation would be worse under a second Trump administration compared to a second Biden term, due in part to tariffs, a crack down on illegal immigration, and larger deficits.[^cite_note-281]

#### Taxation

Biden changed the practice of [IRS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Service) agents disproportionately auditing lower-income Americans.[^cite_note-282]

Finance officials from 130 countries agreed on July 1, 2021, to plans for a new international taxation policy. All the major economies agreed to pass national laws that would require corporations to pay at least 15% income tax in the countries they operate. This new policy would end the practice of locating world headquarters in small countries with very low taxation rates. Governments hope to recoup some of the lost revenue, estimated at \$100 billion to \$240 billion each year. The new system was promoted by the Biden administration and the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development ([OECD](33853117.md)). Secretary-General [Mathias Cormann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathias_Cormann) of the OECD said: "This historic package will ensure that large multinational companies pay their fair share of tax everywhere."[^cite_note-283]

### Energy, environment, and climate

See also: [Environmental policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)



President Biden and Texas governor [Greg Abbott](1492735.md) visit the Harris County Emergency Operations Center in Houston after the [2021 Texas power crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Texas_power_crisis), February 2021.

#### General

During his first week in office, Biden established the position of [White House National Climate Advisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_National_Climate_Advisor), appointing [environmental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_health) and [air quality](10934212.md) expert [Gina McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gina_McCarthy) to the role. Biden also created the position of [U.S. Special Presidential Envoy for Climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Special_Presidential_Envoy_for_Climate), appointing former secretary of state [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry).[^cite_note-284]

On January 20, 2021, Biden signed an executive order rejoining the U.S. to the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md).[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] With the U.S. rejoining the agreement, countries responsible for two-thirds of the global [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) would make pledges of becoming carbon neutral, while without United States it is only half.[^cite_note-287] On the same day, Biden also issued an executive order that cancelled the construction of the [Keystone XL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keystone_XL) pipeline, an extension of the [Keystone Pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keystone_Pipeline). The pipeline was heavily criticized by environmental and Native American activists and groups.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] This order also directed agencies to review and reverse more than 100 actions made by Trump on the environment.[^cite_note-Bradner-118]

On March 27, 2021, Biden invited more than forty world leaders for a climate summit.[^cite_note-290]

In August 2022, Biden signed the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022) into law, a domestic spending bill born out of continued negotiations on the Build Back Better Act after its collapse that fulfilled some of its initial provisions. The bill included significant federal investment in domestic clean energy production, combating climate change, and healthcare; it aims to reduce U.S. carbon emissions by 40% from peak 2005 levels by 2030, included a three-year extension of [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) subsidies, and empowered Medicare to begin negotiating lower prescription drug costs for the first time.[^cite_note-Everett-2022-3]

In May 2022, the [White House Council on Environmental Quality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Council_on_Environmental_Quality) released a report in which it describes how Biden's administration followed the around 200 recommendations of the White House Environmental Justice Advisory Council. The full report has around 150 pages. The report summarizes many of the steps taken by the administration on environmental issues. Among others, it mentions significant achievements in the domains of [energy efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_efficiency_implementation), [weatherization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weatherization), [transit-oriented development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit-oriented_development), [walking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walkability), [cycling](5931.md), [mixed-use development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-use_development), cooperation with [Indigenous peoples of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas).[^cite_note-291]

In April 2024 Biden delivered \$20 billion to eight environmental [NGOs](46539.md). The aim is to reduce the country's CO<sub>2</sub> emissions by 40 million tonnes per year with the money, while giving 70% of the benefits to low income communities.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] In the same month, the [American Climate Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Climate_Corps) is expected to begin function with several hundred members. The tasks will probably be "things like installing solar panels, restoring vulnerable habitats, and fire hazard prevention." Biden plans increase the number of participants to 20,000 during the first year and then 50,000 more will be added each year by 2031. However, this plan is opposed by Republicans.[^cite_note-294]

In May 2024, the administration announced guidelines around carbon markets. They push for increased verifiability from suppliers and transparency from buyers. The guidelines are not binding or enforceable.[^cite_note-295]

In November 2024, the Biden administration withheld federal funding from [Climate Justice Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Justice_Alliance), a move which CJA and [The Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intercept) connected to its support for a ceasefire in Gaza and other progressive policy issues that had made it a target of Republican scrutiny.[^cite_note-296]

#### Oil, gas extraction and transportation

On January 21, 2021, the Biden administration issued a 60-day ban on oil and gas leases and permits on federal land and waters.[^cite_note-297] On January 27, 2021, Biden signed a number of executive orders aimed at [combating climate change](2119179.md),[^cite_note-298] one of them setting climate change as a key consideration for U.S. national security and foreign policy.[^cite_note-abcn_Bideb_says-299] In an attempt to encourage U.S. membership to the [Kigali Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kigali_Amendment), an international agreement aimed to reduce the production of [hydrofluorocarbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrofluorocarbon), Biden's executive order directed the [U.S. Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_State) to submit the Kigali Amendment to the Senate.[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301]



Biden at the [Leaders Summit on Climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Leaders'_Climate_Summit) in April 2021, held [virtually](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_conferencing) because of the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md)

In March 2021, 21 Republican [state attorneys general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_attorneys_general) of 21 states sued the Biden administration for revoking the Keystone XL pipeline permit. The suit claims Biden's executive order exceeded his authority.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303]

In May 2021, the EPA rolled back a Trump administration rule that prohibited the EPA from using certain studies.[^cite_note-Zarrin_Ahmed-304][^cite_note-Frazin-305] The previous rule, which made it more difficult to use major scientific studies to justify pollution reduction policies,[^cite_note-Warren_Cornwall-306] had already been invalidated by a federal court.[^cite_note-Rachel_Frazen-307]

On June 1, 2021, Secretary of the Interior [Deb Haaland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deb_Haaland) suspended all oil and gas drilling leases in Alaska's [Arctic National Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_National_Wildlife_Refuge), pending further review of their environmental impacts.[^cite_note-308]

In January 2021, Biden had issued a 60-day ban on oil and gas leases and permits on federal land and waters. A group of Republican [state attorneys general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_attorneys_general) successfully obtained a decision in federal court to lift the moratorium. The Biden administration appealed the decision but agreed to continue with the sales, and in September 2021 held the largest federal gas and oil lease auction in U.S. history, selling leases to extract 1.7 million [acres in the Gulf of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_oil_and_gas_in_the_Gulf_of_Mexico_\(United_States\)). The areas that were purchased by oil companies can be expected to produce around 4.2 trillion cubic feet of natural gas and 1.12 billion barrels of oil over the next 50 years. The administration has also proposed another round of gas and oil lease sales in 2022, in Colorado, Montana, Wyoming, and other western states.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-StopsShort-311]

The Biden administration supported the [Line 3 pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_3_pipeline), which transports oil from Canada's [oil sands region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_oil_sands).[^cite_note-312]

In November 2021, a closely watched Interior Department report on federal oil and gas lease policy, ordered by Biden, was completed. The report recommended increasing the 12.5% federal royalty rate for oil and gas drilling, which had not been raised by a century, and was significantly lower than rates charged for leasing on state and private land. The report also recommended an increase in the bond rates that drilling companies are required to pay for future cleanup efforts before beginning extraction at new sites, and recommended that leases be focused on sites with "moderate to high potential" for production in proximity to existing fossil-fuel infrastructure.[^cite_note-StopsShort-311] The report stopped short of banning the leasing program, which generates billions of dollars for the federal government, but reformed its terms to be less favorable for industry; environmental groups praised the reforms, but also said they were insufficient to address the U.S. contribution to the [climate crisis](17987598.md).[^cite_note-StopsShort-311]

In 2021, the Biden administration proposed a 20-year ban on oil and gas drilling around [Chaco Culture National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaco_Culture_National_Historical_Park), a site in northwestern New Mexico that contain important [Ancestral Puebloan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestral_Puebloan) sites.[^cite_note-313]

#### Environmental science

In May 2021, the EPA rolled back a Trump administration rule that prohibited the EPA from using certain studies.[^cite_note-Zarrin_Ahmed-304][^cite_note-Frazin-305] The previous rule, which made it more difficult to use major scientific studies to justify pollution reduction policies,[^cite_note-Warren_Cornwall-306] had already been invalidated by a federal court.[^cite_note-Rachel_Frazen-307]

#### Renewable energy

The Biden administration set a goal of achieving 30 gigawatts of [offshore wind energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_wind_energy) generated in the U.S. by 2030 (sufficient to provide electricity to about 10 million homes). In 2021, the Biden administration approved the [South Fork Wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Fork_Wind) project, a major (130 [MW](21347693.md), 12-turbine) commercial [offshore wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_wind_power) project located southeast of [Rhode Island](25410.md)'s [Block Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Block_Island) and east of New York's [Montauk Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montauk_Point), the wind farm is projected to provide electricity to proved 70,000 [Long Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Island) homes. The project is the country's second large-scale offshore wind project (after a [similar wind-power development in Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vineyard_Wind)).[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

#### Nature conservation

The U.S. is part of the [30 by 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_by_30) initiative which aims to preserve 30% of the global land and sea territory by 2030. For the U.S. to accomplish its part, Biden launched a campaign called "America the Beautiful". The campaign seeks to work in cooperation with [indigenous people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) and farmers, as well as better serve disadvantaged communities.[^cite_note-316]

In November 2021, Biden promised to end and reverse [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) and [land degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_degradation) by 2030,[^cite_note-317] joining more than 100 other global leaders in the [COP26 climate summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference)'s first major agreement.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]

According to a report from the [Center for American Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_American_Progress) during the first 3 years of his presidency Biden broke several records in [conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_in_the_United_States), which is "a proven defense against the changing climate". In 2023 alone he turned 12.5 million acres into [protected areas](100406.md) and made 200 agreements with indigenous people about co-management of the protected areas.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] He advanced a proposal to ban logging in [old growth forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-growth_forest) from 2025 which is also important for the climate.[^cite_note-322]

### Electoral and ethical reform

See also: [Electoral and ethics policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_and_ethics_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)

In response to what Biden describes as the growing influence of special interests and [gerrymandering](12987.md) in elections, he has pledged to seek electoral and government ethics reforms.[^cite_note-govtreform-323] Biden supported the [For the People Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_the_People_Act) and the [John Lewis Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis_Voting_Rights_Act).[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] In January 2022, he endorsed a change to [senate filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster_in_the_United_States_Senate) rules after they both failed to invoke [cloture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloture).[^cite_note-326] However, the rules change failed when two Democratic senators joined Senate Republicans in opposing it.[^cite_note-\:5b-327]

Known for his generally bipartisan tone, Biden avoided directly referring to his predecessor during his first year in office.[^cite_note-StrongAlarm-328] Beginning in 2022, Biden condemned Trump and [Trumpism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism) in stronger terms; he likened the "MAGA philosophy" to "semi-fascism" and, in [a 2022 speech outside Independence Hall in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_for_the_Soul_of_the_Nation_speech), said the "extreme ideology" of Trump, and a Republican Party dominated by him, "threatens the very foundation of our republic." Biden specifically condemned Trump and "MAGA Republicans" for promoting authoritarian leaders, using violent rhetoric, refusing to disavow [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence), and refusing to acknowledge election losses.[^cite_note-StrongAlarm-328][^cite_note-329] Biden suggested that the [2022 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections) could be illegitimate if federal laws are not passed to combat enacted voter-suppression legislation from state legislatures.[^cite_note-Elections-330][^cite_note-Elections2-331][^cite_note-Elections3-332]

After the [attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md), Biden called for reforms to the 1887 [Electoral Count Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Count_Act) to clarify the roles of Congress and the vice president in certifying electoral votes. The [Electoral Count Reform and Presidential Transition Improvement Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Count_Reform_and_Presidential_Transition_Improvement_Act_of_2022) raised the threshold for objections to electoral votes, clarified that the vice president cannot decertify electoral votes, and modified the process for which electors are certified. It was included as part of the [2023 Consolidated Appropriations Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_Appropriations_Act,_2023).[^cite_note-333]

### Housing

Main article: [Housing Supply Action Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_Supply_Action_Plan)

Due to ongoing decreases in housing affordability exacerbated by the pandemic, inflation, and snarls in the global supply chain, Biden launched the [Housing Supply Action Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_Supply_Action_Plan) on May 16, 2022, to increase housing supply and construction through executive initiatives, administrative deregulation and funding from the [Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act). Due to the statutory limits upon federal intervention in housing policy, the plan relied heavily upon partnerships with willing municipal governments and the private sector to reform [zoning and permitting laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoning_in_the_United_States) and secure construction supply, issuing financial incentives to municipal governments for cooperation with the plan, issuing financial support to renters, landlords and first-time homeowners, and disincentivizing large institutional investors from acquiring government-owned or funded home properties.[^cite_note-334] Biden also called upon Congress to pass an Unlocking Possibilities Program to allow [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development) to issue grants to states and localities to help reform permitting for affordable housing, as well as further mortgage assistance for first-time homebuyers.[^cite_note-335]

Through the [Inflation Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act), the Biden administration secured over \$25 billion to invest in a variety of programs and tax credits that incentivize renovations and energy efficiency in housing through five federal agencies ([HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development), [IRS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Service), [DOE](62866.md), [DOT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Transportation), [EPA](58666.md)).[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]

### Immigration

See also: [Immigration policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_policy_of_the_Biden_administration) and [Mexico–United States border crisis § Biden administration (2021–2025)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_crisis#Biden_administration_\(2021%E2%80%932025\))



Presidential Proclamation 10141 – Ending Discriminatory Bans on Entry to the United States

On January 20, 2021, the day Biden was inaugurated, he halted the construction of the [U.S.–Mexico barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93Mexico_barrier)[^cite_note-Bradner-118] and ended [a related national emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Emergency_Concerning_the_Southern_Border_of_the_United_States) declared by Trump in February 2018.[^cite_note-Everett-2022-3] Biden issued a proclamation that ended the [Trump travel ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_travel_ban) imposed on predominantly Muslim countries in January 2017.[^cite_note-Bradner-118][^cite_note-Everett-2022-3] Biden also reaffirmed protections to recipients of [Deferred Action for Childhood Arrivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deferred_Action_for_Childhood_Arrivals) (DACA).[^cite_note-Narea-338] The same day, Biden sent a memorandum to the [U.S. Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_State) reinstating Deferred Enforced Departure for Liberians.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]

On January 20, Biden proposed the [U.S. Citizenship Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Citizenship_Act_of_2021) which would enact broad changes to the immigration system.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-\:0-342] It would provide an eight-year potential pathway for citizenship to eleven million immigrants living in the U.S. without a permanent legal status.[^cite_note-\:0-342][^cite_note-Hackman-2021-343] The bill would also make it easier for certain foreign workers to stay in the U.S.[^cite_note-Fram-2021-344][^cite_note-Spagat-2021-345] In addition, it would deliver record budget allocations for the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) to secure the border and \$4 billion to El Salvador, Guatemala and Honduras to combat the root causes of their emigration.[^cite_note-346] If passed, it would also replace the word "alien" with "noncitizen" in [U.S. immigration law](18455370.md).[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349] [Senate Majority Whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Majority_Whip) [Dick Durbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Durbin) called the bill "aspirational" and it was widely expected not to pass both houses of Congress without significant revision.[^cite_note-Hackman-2021-343][^cite_note-Fram-2021-344][^cite_note-Spagat-2021-345]

Also on January 20, 2021, the Biden administration issued a moratorium on [deportations](412990.md) from the [U.S. Department of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Homeland_Security) (DHS) for the first 100 days of his presidency.[^cite_note-350] On January 22, [Texas Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Attorney_General) [Ken Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Paxton) sued the Biden administration for violating Biden's written pledge to cooperatively work with the state of Texas.[^cite_note-351] A federal judge in Texas subsequently issued a temporary restraining order barring the Biden administration from enforcing its moratorium, citing the lack of "any concrete, reasonable justification for a 100-day pause on deportations."[^cite_note-apne_Judge_bars-352]

On January 22, 2021, Biden had a call with [Mexican president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_president) [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador). On the call, Biden and López Obrador spoke about immigration, where Biden spoke of reducing [immigration from Mexico to the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_Mexico) by targeting what Biden deemed as root causes.[^cite_note-Obradorcall-353] According to an [Associated Press](18935732.md) report, López Obrador noted that Biden pledged \$4 billion to "help development in Honduras, El Salvador and Guatemala — nations whose hardships have spawned tides of migration through Mexico toward the United States."[^cite_note-354]

According to a 2021 *Politico* report, Republicans expected prior to Biden taking office that there would be a border surge at the start of 2021 (due to seasonal patterns and regional crises) and coordinated to make it a central issue in the lead-up to the 2022 mid-term elections.[^cite_note-355] The number of migrants arriving in the U.S. from Central America had been rising since April 2020 due to ongoing violence, natural disasters, food insecurity, and poverty in the region.[^cite_note-356] In February 2021, the [U.S. Border Patrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Border_Patrol) reported a 61% increase in encounters with unaccompanied children from the month before, the largest one-month percentage increase in encounters with unaccompanied children since [U.S. Customs and Border Protection](757040.md) began recording data in 2010.[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358]

Biden instructed the [U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Immigration_and_Customs_Enforcement) (ICE) to focus on violent offenders of immigration laws rather than all offenders of immigration laws.[^cite_note-Kanno-Youngs-2021-359][^cite_note-360]

In February 2021, it was reported that DHS agents who had been empowered by Trump to enact his anti-immigration policies were resisting and defying Biden's immigration policies.[^cite_note-Kanno-Youngs-2021-359] The union representing ICE agents signaled that its agents would not accept reversals of Trump policies.[^cite_note-Kanno-Youngs-2021-359]

&#x20;In March 2021, the Biden administration granted [temporary protected status](20497716.md) to Venezuelans [fleeing the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_refugee_crisis) amidst the ongoing political and economic [crisis](52966258.md).[^cite_note-361]



Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) meets with State Department Special Envoy for the Northern Triangle, Ricardo Zúñiga, and other officials on the surge of migrants from Central America, March 2021.

On March 24, 2021, Biden tasked Vice President Harris to reduce the number of [unaccompanied minors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unaccompanied_minor) and adult [asylum seekers](276005.md). She is also tasked with leading the negotiations with [Mexico](3966054.md), [Honduras](13394.md), [Guatemala](17238567.md), and [El Salvador](9356.md).[^cite_note-362] The number of migrant families and unaccompanied children entering the U.S. from across the Southwest border steeply declined in August, September, and October 2021.[^cite_note-363]

On June 1, 2021, the DHS officially terminated the [Trump](52231341.md)-era "[Remain in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_policy_of_the_first_Donald_Trump_administration#Restrictions_on_asylum)" policy, which mandated that all [asylum seekers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asylum_seekers) from Central America were to wait in Mexico pending their court cases; however, a health order from March 2020 allowed the border authorities to send migrants back for the duration of the COVID-19 pandemic have remained in place.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] However, on August 14, 2021, a federal judge in Texas ordered the Biden administration to reimplement the policy; the [Supreme Court](31737.md) placed a pause on the ruling to give the administration time for arguments.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] On August 24, 2021, the Supreme Court ruled that the Biden administration must comply with the lower court's ruling to reinstate the policy.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369]

In fiscal year 2022, over one million [immigrants](15051.md) (most of whom entered through [family reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_migration#Legislation_and_chain_migration)) were granted [legal residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_residence_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-370] up from 707,000 in 2020.[^cite_note-371] Up until 2022 during Biden's presidency, the number of unauthorized immigrants in the United States steadily rose.[^cite_note-372]

On October 5, 2023, Biden's administration said it will add sections to a border wall to stave off record migrant crossings from Mexico, carrying forward a signature policy of former president Donald Trump.[^cite_note-reuters-373][^cite_note-374] One of Biden's first actions after taking office in January 2021 was to issue a proclamation pledging that "no more American taxpayer dollars be diverted to construct a border wall" as well as a review of all resources that had already been committed. The administration said Thursday's action did not deviate from Biden's proclamation because money that was allocated during Trump's term in 2019 had to be spent now.[^cite_note-reuters-373] Former president Trump was quick to claim victory and demand an apology: "As I have stated often, over thousands of years, there are only two things that have consistently worked, wheels, and walls!" Trump wrote on social media. "Will Joe Biden apologize to me and America for taking so long to get moving...".[^cite_note-reuters-373] Mexico's President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador called the move "a step backwards."[^cite_note-reuters-373]

On October 31, 2023, Homeland Security Secretary [Alejandro Mayorkas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Mayorkas) testified before the Senate Homeland Security Committee that more than 600,000 people illegally made their way into the United States without being apprehended by border agents during the 2023 fiscal year.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

President Biden pledged to welcome 125,000 [refugees](7195971.md) in 2024.[^cite_note-377] The Biden administration considered accepting [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) from the [Gaza Strip](12047.md).[^cite_note-378]

On March 29, 2024, in response to a class-action lawsuit filed by lawyers representing detained migrant children, the Biden administration argued in front of a federal court that it had no responsibility to feed and house migrant children that were waiting in Border Patrol camps along the U.S.-Mexico border.[^cite_note-379] This argument was rejected by Judge Dolly M. Gee, who ordered the administration to "expeditiously" house all detained migrant children.[^cite_note-380]

On June 4, 2024, President Biden issued a new executive order granting immigration officials the authority to deport certain asylum seekers without processing their claims. This measure targets migrants who enter the United States "without permission and against the law," making it easier for authorities to deport them.[^cite_note-381] Under the new order, if migrants express fear of returning to their home countries, they may be given an interview with an asylum officer. However, agents are no longer required to ask migrants if they have such fears.[^cite_note-382] As part of the new action, the Biden administration announced the closure of the [South Texas Family Residential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Texas_Family_Residential_Center), the largest immigrant detention center in the United States. The primary reason cited for this decision was the high cost of operating the facility.[^cite_note-383]

#### 2024 bipartisan border bill

This section is an excerpt from [Mexico–United States border crisis § 2024 bipartisan border bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_crisis#2024_bipartisan_border_bill).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_crisis\&action=edit)]

Following months of negotiations, on February 4, 2024, a bipartisan group of senators released a 370-page bill intended to sharply reduce incentives for migrants to attempt border crossings. The bill included a "border emergency" provision that would automatically require the border to be closed if border encounters reached an average of 5,000 per day over several days. Trump, [Speaker Mike Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_Mike_Johnson), and other Republicans falsely claimed the bill allowed 5,000 illegal border crossings per day.[^cite_note-Mexico–United_States_border_crisis_5000nbc-384] In reality, the bill would have ended the practice of ["catch and release"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catch_and_release_\(immigration\)) that allows migrants entry into the country while they await immigration hearings; instead, migrants would have been [detained](27272402.md) pending hearings.[^cite_note-Mexico–United_States_border_crisis_5000nbc-384] The plan included a tighter asylum application and approval process with speedy removal of migrants who do not qualify, the hiring of thousands more border patrol and asylum officers and an increase in detention capacity. The bill also provided for thousands of work visas for migrant spouses of U.S. citizens awaiting immigrant visas, and 250,000 new visas over five years for people seeking to work in the U.S. or join family members. It aimed to provide a [pathway to citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathway_to_citizenship) for "Documented Dreamers", children who accompanied their parents on a work visa and who could lose their place in line for a [green card](300237.md) at age 21.[^cite_note-Mexico–United_States_border_crisis_a583-385]

President Biden supported the bill, while Speaker Johnson said days before its release that it would be "dead on arrival" in the Republican-controlled House. Shortly after the proposal was announced, [House majority leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_majority_leader) [Steve Scalise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Scalise) declared it would not be brought before the House for a vote. One negotiator, independent [Arizona](21883824.md) Senator [Kyrsten Sinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrsten_Sinema), asserted the border would have been closed every day so far in early January 2024 if the bill had been law; Biden promised in January that he would immediately close the border if the bill was enacted. Lead Republican negotiator [James Lankford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Lankford) of Oklahoma, among the most conservative Republican Senators, attempted to defend the bill throughout the weeks leading to the vote. Trump, the leading Republican candidate for president in 2024, publicly and privately pressured Republicans during the Senate negotiations to oppose the proposal. The [National Border Patrol Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Border_Patrol_Council), a labor union representing some 18,000 border patrol officers, quickly endorsed the Senate bill. The union had endorsed Trump in 2020 and had been critical of Biden's policies.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390]

Senate Republicans swiftly turned against the bill upon its release, after Trump openly said he did not want Joe Biden to score a political win with the legislation.[^cite_note-391] On February 7, 2024, Senate Republicans blocked the proposal in a floor vote. Lankford said on the floor before the vote that a "popular commentator" had told him a month earlier, "'If you try to move a bill that solves the border crisis during this presidential year, I will do whatever I can to destroy you, because I do not want you to solve this during the presidential election.'" Two days before the vote, Trump told a radio host, "This is a very bad bill for his career."[^cite_note-392] Trump said at a rally days later, "We crushed crooked Joe Biden's disastrous open borders bill," while Biden said, "Every day between now and November, the American people are going to know that the only reason the border is not secure is Donald Trump and his [MAGA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Make_America_Great_Again) Republican friends."[^cite_note-393]

### Separation of church and state

Biden, a practicing Catholic,[^cite_note-394] has taken a public position of dissent against the Church's position opposing free-choice in the abortion issue.[^cite_note-Brian_Bennett_2021-395] This has raised the question of whether his public office might allow him to influence the outcomes of current debates with the Church concerning abortion.[^cite_note-396] The Vatican has taken a mediating position concerning Biden's dissent by allowing him to take [Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist) in Rome while visiting the Pope.[^cite_note-Brian_Bennett_2021-395]

### Social issues

Main article: [Social policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)



President Biden signs executive orders expanding the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) and revoking [Trump administration](52231341.md) health policies, January 2021.



President Biden signed the [Juneteenth National Independence Day Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth_National_Independence_Day_Act) into law, June 17, 2021.

During his early days in office, Biden focused on "advancing equity, civil rights, racial justice and equal opportunity." According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, Biden's early actions in office focused on [racial equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_equality) more than any president since [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson), who passed the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964).[^cite_note-397] On January 25, 2021, Biden signed an executive order that lifted the [ban on transgender military service members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Memorandum_on_Military_Service_by_Transgender_Individuals_\(2017\)).[^cite_note-398] This reversed a memorandum imposed by Trump.[^cite_note-399]

The Biden administration sought to put [Harriet Tubman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Tubman) on the [twenty-dollar bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_twenty-dollar_bill).[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401] This effort followed that of the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama), which was blocked by [Steven Mnuchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Mnuchin).[^cite_note-402] Press secretary Psaki said it was important that [U.S. money and notes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Note) reflect the "history and diversity" of the country and that putting Tubman on the twenty-dollar bill would do so.[^cite_note-403]

On January 20, 2021, the Biden administration issued an Executive Order entitled Advancing Racial Equity and Support for Underserved Communities Through the Federal Government[^cite_note-404] increasing the federal government's anti-bias enforcement against government contractors. The intent is heightened investigations and audits by the [Department of Justice](52563.md) as well as more detailed follow-up inquiries with government contractors, with an emphasis on combatting pay discrimination.[^cite_note-405] Also in January, Biden directed the U.S. Department of Justice to reduce their usage of [private prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_prison) and ordered the attorney general to not renew contracts with private prisons, citing the need to "reduce profit-based incentives" for the incarceration of racial minorities.[^cite_note-theh_Biden_directs-406][^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408]

Three days after the [Atlanta spa shootings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Atlanta_spa_shootings) that killed 8 people, including 6 Asian women, Biden and Vice President Harris travelled to [Atlanta](3138.md). They spoke to Asian American and Pacific Islander advocates and leaders and condemned sexism, and racism against Asian Americans.[^cite_note-409]

Biden made [Juneteenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth) (June 19) a federal holiday in 2021, celebrating the end of slavery in the U.S.[^cite_note-410] In March 2022, Biden signed the [Emmett Till Antilynching Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till_Antilynching_Act) into law. With the enactment of that legislation, [lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching) was made a federal [hate crime](13547.md) for the first time in American history.[^cite_note-411]

In October 2022, [Biden pardoned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_policy_of_the_Biden_administration) all past federal marijuana possession charges and announced an inquiry into whether [cannabis should be removed from Schedule I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Removal_of_cannabis_from_Schedule_I_of_the_Controlled_Substances_Act) of the [Controlled Substances Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled_Substances_Act).[^cite_note-412] In December 2022, Biden signed the [Medical Marijuana and Cannabidiol Research Expansion Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_Marijuana_and_Cannabidiol_Research_Expansion_Act) which was the first standalone cannabis-related bill ever passed by the United States Congress.[^cite_note-413]

In January 2025, Biden declared the lapsed [Equal Rights Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Rights_Amendment) ratified as the "28th Amendment" to the [constitution](31644.md). However, the declaration has no formal effect[^cite_note-Stokols-414][^cite_note-415] and the [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_of_the_United_States) has said they do not intend to certify the amendment as part of the constitution, citing "established legal, judicial, and procedural decisions."[^cite_note-416]

#### LGBT rights

The [United States Department of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Agriculture) unveiled efforts in 2022 to prevent [anti-LGBT discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-LGBT_discrimination_in_the_United_States) in food programs, including [school lunch programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_School_Lunch_Act), resulting in 20 lawsuits from 20 Republican [attorneys general](6065438.md).[^cite_note-417]

In December 2022, Biden signed the [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act), which repealed the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) (DOMA), required states to recognize other states' marriage certificates for [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT) Americans, and ensured some religious liberties.[^cite_note-418] The bill came from a push from Democrats to codify same-sex marriage after the [Dobbs decision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization), particularly [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas)'s push to reconsider other [precedents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedent).[^cite_note-419]

In January 2023, the Biden administration released an "evidence agenda" for [LGBTQI+ Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_people_in_the_United_States) to learn about "their experiences engaging with federal agencies and the disparities they face in their daily lives," as well as documenting how many LGBT people live in the United States.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421]

#### Abortion

In December 2021, the Biden administration ended a long-standing restriction on sales of abortion pills through the mail. This decision came amidst legal cases and Supreme Court decisions that jeopardized abortion access in the United States.[^cite_note-422]

After the U.S. Supreme Court overturned *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* and *[Planned Parenthood v. Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_Parenthood_v._Casey)* on June 24, 2022, in *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization),* Biden addressed the nation in the Cross Hall of the White House.[^cite_note-whitehouse1-423][^cite_note-c-span1-424] He mentioned that "it's a sad day ... for the country" and "with Roe gone, ... The health and life of women in this nation are now at risk."[^cite_note-whitehouse1-423][^cite_note-c-span1-424] In addition, he attacked the Court saying "With this decision, the conservative majority of the Supreme Court shows how extreme it is" and "They have made the United States an outlier among developed nations in the world."[^cite_note-whitehouse1-423][^cite_note-c-span1-424] Regarding action, Biden stated that his administration will defend the right of women to seek an abortion in another state where abortion is legal and help protect a woman's access to contraception and abortion pills approved by the FDA.[^cite_note-whitehouse1-423][^cite_note-c-span1-424] He also called on Congress to codify *Roe v. Wade*, saying "No executive action ... can do that.".[^cite_note-whitehouse1-423][^cite_note-c-span1-424] But stated that if Congress did not have the votes to codify, that the voters would have to take action by "elect\[ing] more senators and representatives who will codify a woman's right to choose into federal law."[^cite_note-whitehouse1-423][^cite_note-c-span1-424]

The Biden administration rejected the call from [progressives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States)[^cite_note-425] to allow abortions on federal land, with White House press secretary [Karine Jean-Pierre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karine_Jean-Pierre) saying, "in states where abortion is now illegal, women and providers who are not federal employees, as you look at the federal land, could be potentially – be prosecuted."[^cite_note-426]

During a press conference at the [2022 Madrid NATO Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Madrid_summit), Biden expressed support for providing an exception to the filibuster to codify *Roe v. Wade.*[^cite_note-427]

#### Criminal justice and crime rates

The Biden administration rescinded a Trump administration policy that curtailed the use of [consent decrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_decree) that had been used by previous administrations in their investigations of misconduct in police departments.[^cite_note-428]

Biden proposed in his fiscal 2022 budget to more than double funding for the Community Oriented Policing Services (COPS) Hiring Program, which helps state and local governments to hire law enforcement officers.[^cite_note-429]

Despite perceptions, notably by Republicans and conservative media, of a "crime crisis" of soaring violent crime under Biden, FBI data indicated the violent crime rate had declined significantly during the president's first two years in office, after a spike in 2020 during the [COVID pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID_pandemic). By 2022, the violent crime rate was near a 50-year low, and preliminary data released in early 2024 indicated continuing declines in 2023.[^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433]

As of July 1, 2024, violent crime was down and homicides were on pace to drop to 2015 levels by the end of the year.[^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435]

#### Pardons and commutations

See also: [List of people pardoned or granted clemency by the president of the United States § Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_pardoned_or_granted_clemency_by_the_president_of_the_United_States#Joe_Biden)

Biden issued more individual pardons and commutations than any other president.[^cite_note-APpardons-11] On October 6, 2022, he pardoned all Americans convicted of "small" amounts of [cannabis](1481886.md) possession under federal law.[^cite_note-436] In December 2023, he pardoned Americans for cannabis use or possession on federal lands regardless of whether they had been charged or prosecuted.[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-438] In December 2024, in the largest single-day clemency act in history, Biden granted clemency to about 1,500 nonviolent felons in [home confinement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_confinement) who had previously been released from prison.[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440] The act generated controversy, as it included felons such as [Michael Conahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Conahan), a judge involved in the [kids for cash kickback scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kids_for_cash), and [Rita Crundwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rita_Crundwell), a comptroller responsible for the single largest municipal fraud in U.S. history.[^cite_note-441] Also in December 2024, Biden commuted the sentences of 37 of the 40 individuals on [federal death row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_by_the_United_States_federal_government) to life imprisonment without the possibility of parole.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443]

&#x20;On December 1, 2024, Biden issued a "full and unconditional" pardon to his son, Hunter Biden, that covered all federal offenses between 2014 and December 1, 2024. According to [*Reason*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason_\(magazine\)) magazine, the pardon's sweeping extent was "unprecedented" and was even more far-reaching than [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon)'s or other "controversial" pardons:[^cite_note-Reason-444]

> The Hunter pardon is far more comprehensive...in that it covered not just his convictions for drug-related activities and tax fraud, but *any other criminal behavior* since 2014—the year that Hunter joined the board of Ukrainian energy company Burisma. It has been alleged that Hunter's job was essentially to trade on the family name and sell his access to dad. This may not have been illegal, but it does mean that the pardon is clearly designed to offer preemptive protection not just to Hunter, but to Joe Biden himself. These features make the pardon unprecedented, though perfectly in line with the president's executive powers.

Hunter had been convicted on charges related to tax and gun offenses, after which Joe made numerous promises not to pardon him. He and his staff continued to say that Hunter would not be pardoned as late as November, although internal staff discussions affirmed that the option remained on the table even as Biden said otherwise.[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446][^cite_note-NBCpardon-447] In a statement announcing the pardon, Biden said he believed his son was "selectively, and unfairly, prosecuted".[^cite_note-448]

On his last day in office, Biden issued pardons for more of his family members and other high-profile figures.[^cite_note-lastpardonnyt-449] The pardons covered Biden's siblings and their spouses, including [James Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Biden), who was interviewed as part of an impeachment probe into Biden. Others pardoned that day include former [chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff](364458.md) [Mark Milley](38091005.md), former [National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases](1013155.md) director [Anthony Fauci](1227250.md), and members and participants in the [House Select Committee on the January 6 Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Select_Committee_on_the_January_6_Attack), despite many of those pardoned not having been under criminal investigation.[^cite_note-lastpardonnyt-449][^cite_note-APpardons-11] Biden justified the pardons by citing his concern about "baseless and politically motivated investigations" during Trump's second term.[^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452] Biden added that the pardons were preemptive and should not be seen as implying their recipients' guilt.[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-454] The pardons Biden granted to his family and other political figures had a sweeping scope similar to the one he granted Hunter, covering federal offenses the recipients committed or may have committed between 2014 and the day of the pardon.[^cite_note-APpardons-11][^cite_note-455]

#### Gun control



President Biden announces new executive measures on gun control with Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) and Attorney General [Merrick Garland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick_Garland) in the [White House Rose Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Rose_Garden), April 8, 2021.

President Biden addresses the nation after the [Robb Elementary School shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robb_Elementary_School_shooting).

In a national address in March 2021, after [mass shootings](50822291.md) in the [Atlanta area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Atlanta_spa_shootings) and [Boulder, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Boulder_shooting), Biden advocated further gun regulations, such as a restored [ban on assault weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assault_weapons_legislation_in_the_United_States) and a [high-capacity magazine ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-capacity_magazine_ban), as well as reinforcing preexisting background checks.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457]

After the [Robb Elementary School shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robb_Elementary_School_shooting) on May 24, 2022, President Biden addressed the nation.[^cite_note-458] The next week, he again called on Congress to pass an assault weapons and high-capacity magazine ban, as well as [red flag laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_flag_law) and other legislation.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460] As a result of the shooting, the [Bipartisan Safer Communities Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipartisan_Safer_Communities_Act) was eventually passed by Congress and signed into law. It marked the first federal gun safety law to have been enacted in 30 years.[^cite_note-461]

After the [Colorado Springs nightclub shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Springs_nightclub_shooting), Biden called again for an assault weapons ban.[^cite_note-462]

### Space policy

The Biden administration reversed the Trump administration's method of using the [National Space Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Space_Council) to coordinate commercial, civil, and military space policies, instead using the [National Security Council](44026.md) to issue national security memoranda instead of the Space Council's space policy directives.[^cite_note-463] The Biden administration renewed the National Space Council, chaired by Vice President Harris,[^cite_note-FisherSpace-464] "to assist the president in generating national space policies, strategies, and synchronizing America's space activities."[^cite_note-465] Harris held meetings with the leaders of five countries to discuss [international cooperation on space issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_outer_space).[^cite_note-FisherSpace-464]

The Biden administration continued the [Artemis program](60758751.md) to send people back [to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing).[^cite_note-FisherSpace-464][^cite_note-466] The administration also emphasized the role of NASA in studying [climate change](5042951.md).[^cite_note-FisherSpace-464][^cite_note-BenderCustodio-467]

Biden appointed [Bill Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Nelson), an astronaut and former U.S. senator, to the post of [NASA administrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA_administrator). Nelson was confirmed unanimously by the Senate in April 2021.[^cite_note-468]

In April 2021, as part of his first annual budget request, Biden proposed a \$24.8 billion budget for NASA in 2022, a \$1.5 billion increase on what Congress allocated to 2021.[^cite_note-BenderCustodio-467][^cite_note-Space.com1-469] The proposal includes funding for the Artemis program for a new crewed [Moon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing) mission.[^cite_note-Space.com1-469] The proposal also included a 12.5% increase for NASA's Earth Science Division, as well as a 22% increase for the [National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration](37876.md), which operates a fleet of [weather satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weather_satellite); both measures aimed to use space tools to study and [mitigate climate change](2119179.md).[^cite_note-BenderCustodio-467]

On December 1, 2021, Biden announced his new framework for US space policy, the United States Space Priorities Framework, which explains Biden's approach for commercial, civil, and military space activity.[^cite_note-The_White_House-2021-470] There is a new emphasis on combating climate change and investing in [STEM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science,_technology,_engineering,_and_mathematics) education.[^cite_note-The_White_House-2021-470]

### Surveillance

In 2024, Biden reauthorized [Section 702](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_702).[^cite_note-471]

## Foreign affairs

See also: [List of visits by foreign leaders to the United States under Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_visits_by_foreign_leaders_to_the_United_States_under_Joe_Biden) and [Antony Blinken § Foreign policy positions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken#Foreign_policy_positions)



President Biden signs his first bill, H.R. 335.

Main article: [Foreign policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)



As of December 2024, [Biden had made 21 international trips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_presidential_trips_made_by_Joe_Biden) to 28 different countries during his presidency.

### Defense

On January 22, 2021, Biden signed his first bill,[^cite_note-Hoffman-472] H.R. 335 into law, providing a waiver to the law preventing appointment of a [Secretary of Defense](43998.md) who had been on active duty in the [armed forces](32087.md) within the past seven years;[^cite_note-473] this was the third time such a waiver was granted by Congress. Retired army [four-star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four-star_rank) [general](2965165.md) [Lloyd Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Austin) was confirmed by the [Senate](24909346.md) in a 93–2 vote that same day, making Austin the first [African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American) Defense Secretary.[^cite_note-474][^cite_note-Hoffman-472]



President Biden delivers remarks at [The Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon), February 2021.

Austin said his number one priority was to assist [COVID-19](63136490.md) relief efforts, pledging he would "quickly review the Department's contributions to COVID-19 relief efforts, ensuring that we're doing everything that we can to help distribute vaccines across the country and to vaccinate our troops and preserve readiness."[^cite_note-475]

On February 10, 2021, Biden visited [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) for the first time as president.[^cite_note-476] In remarks to service members alongside Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) and Defense Secretary Lloyd Austin, Biden announced a [U.S. Department of Defense](7279897.md)-led China task force "to provide a baseline assessment of department policies, programs and processes in regard to the challenge China poses."[^cite_note-477]

On June 18, 2021, the administration removed eight [MIM-104 Patriot](52024.md) anti-missile batteries from [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), [Jordan](7515964.md), [Kuwait](7515890.md), and [Iraq](7515928.md), removed the [THAAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/THAAD) anti-missile defense system from Saudi Arabia, and announced that most jet squadrons and hundreds of [American troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_troops) would be withdrawn from the region. The changes come in light of both de-escalating tensions with Iran and the administration changing its focus on countering China.[^cite_note-478]

After taking office, Biden heavily restricted the use of [armed drones](337755.md) and drone strikes.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480] After Biden's first year in office, drone strikes had hit a 20-year low and were heavily limited by the administration.[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-482]



Biden and Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) at the welcoming ceremony for Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff [Charles Q. Brown Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Q._Brown_Jr.) (left) on September 29, 2023. Outgoing chairman General [Mark Milley](38091005.md) (right) and Secretary of Defense [Lloyd Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Austin) (center-right) are present.

In October 2023, President Biden asked Congress for nearly \$106 billion in funding for Israel, Ukraine, countering China in the Indo-Pacific, and operations on the southern U.S. border.[^cite_note-483] Biden signed a record \$886 billion [defense spending bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Authorization_Act_for_Fiscal_Year_2024) into law on December 22, 2023.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485]

### Indo-Pacific strategy



President Biden hosted the Quad meeting at the White House, September 24, 2021.



President Biden hosted the [American–Japanese–Korean trilateral pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American%E2%80%93Japanese%E2%80%93Korean_trilateral_pact) at [Camp David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_David), August 18, 2023.

In 2021, Biden held a virtual meeting with leaders of [Japan](15573.md), [India](14533.md) and [Australia](4689264.md), an alliance of countries known as the [Quadrilateral Security Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue), or the Quad, that work together to address China's expansionism in the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md) region.[^cite_note-wsj._Biden_Meets-486][^cite_note-wsj._U.S._India-487] This was followed by high level meetings and sanctions on Chinese officials,[^cite_note-npr._Blinken_Austin-488][^cite_note-wsj._Bitter_Alaska-489][^cite_note-wsj._U.S._India-487] and later hosting the first in-person Quad summit at the White House.[^cite_note-490]

On May 23, 2022, Biden launched the [Indo-Pacific Economic Framework for Prosperity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pacific_Economic_Framework_for_Prosperity) (IPEF) with 12 regional partners to counter growing Chinese economic and political influence in the Indo-Pacific region,[^cite_note-491] a move China described as a "closed and exclusive club".[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493] In November 2023, under pressure from Congressional Democrats, the Biden administration halted plans for the IPEF's trade component.[^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495]

On August 18, 2023, Biden hosted Japan's [Fumio Kishida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumio_Kishida) and South Korea's [Yoon Suk Yeol](62877952.md) at [Camp David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_David), producing the [Camp David Principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American%E2%80%93Japanese%E2%80%93Korean_trilateral_pact) relating to joint military exercises, [supply chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chain) resilience, and united criticism of North Korea, Russia, and China.[^cite_note-496]

### China

Biden has said the U.S. needs to "get tough" on China and build "a united front of U.S. allies and partners to confront China's abusive behaviors and human rights violations."[^cite_note-fore_Why_America-497] He described China as the "most serious competitor" that poses challenges on the "prosperity, security, and democratic values" of the U.S.[^cite_note-whit_Remarks_by-498]

Biden nominated [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) to serve as Secretary of State who took office on January 26, 2021.[^cite_note-499][^cite_note-Bernstein-2021-500] During his nomination hearing, Blinken said that previous optimistic approaches to China were flawed,[^cite_note-wsj._Blinken_Backs-501] and that Biden's predecessor, Donald Trump, "was right in taking a tougher approach to China" but he "disagree\[s] very much with the way \[Trump] went about it in a number of areas."[^cite_note-Bernstein-2021-500] He endorsed former secretary of state [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo)'s report that China is committing a [genocide against Uyghur Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China).[^cite_note-Bernstein-2021-500]



Secretary of State [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) meets with Chinese [State Councillor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_councillor) and Foreign Minister [Wang Yi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Yi_\(politician\)) on October 31, 2021.

In March 2021, Secretary of State Antony Blinken, [National Security Advisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_security_advisor) [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan) and other administration officials met with the [Chinese Communist Party Politburo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party_Politburo) member [Yang Jiechi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_Jiechi), Chinese foreign minister [Wang Yi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Yi_\(politician\)), and other Chinese officials in [Alaska](624.md) with heated exchanges on [China's human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_China) abuses, [cyberattacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_cyberwarfare), its threats against [Taiwan](25734.md), its crackdown in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) and [Hong Kong](13404.md), and other issues of U.S. interest. The Chinese side countered: "The U.S. does not have the qualification to speak to China from a position of strength \[and] does not serve as a model to others \[and] China's development and strengthening is unstoppable."[^cite_note-wsj._Bitter_Alaska-489][^cite_note-502]

On October 20, 2021, Biden said he is concerned about [Chinese hypersonic missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypersonic_flight), days after China tested a [nuclear-capable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) hypersonic missile that circled the globe before speeding towards its target.[^cite_note-503]



Biden meets with [Chinese leader](262766.md) [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) at the [G20 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_G20_Bali_summit) in [Bali](4147.md) on November 14, 2022.

The U.S. took steps to [restrict TikTok](73356801.md) over national-security concerns related to its Chinese ownership. In December 2022, Biden signed the [No TikTok on Government Devices Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_TikTok_on_Government_Devices_Act), barring [TikTok](56822861.md) on federal devices.[^cite_note-504] In April 2024, he signed the [Protecting Americans from Foreign Adversary Controlled Applications Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protecting_Americans_from_Foreign_Adversary_Controlled_Applications_Act),[^cite_note-505] which set a deadline for apps owned by [foreign adversaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_foreign_adversaries#Other_meanings), such as TikTok's parent company [ByteDance](54450737.md), to either be sold to a non-adversary owner or be banned in the United States. TikTok's U.S. ban was not enforced; service was restored and the enforcement deadline was repeatedly extended.[^cite_note-506]

### Taiwan

On September 18, 2022, Biden said that U.S. forces would defend Taiwan if China launched an invasion. China condemned the remark, saying it sent the wrong signal to supporters of Taiwanese independence. The policy was stated in contrast to Biden's previous exclusion of U.S. forces in support for Ukraine in its current conflict with Russia.[^cite_note-507]

### Myanmar

On February 1, 2021, Biden condemned the [Myanmar coup d'état](66567997.md) and called for the release of detained officials. Biden also left open the door to re-imposing sanctions on the country, saying in a statement that "\[t]he United States removed sanctions on Burma over the past decade based on progress toward democracy. The reversal of that progress will necessitate an immediate review of our sanction laws and authorities, followed by appropriate action."[^cite_note-508]

On March 5, 2021, Biden imposed sanctions on Myanmar's [Ministry of Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_\(Myanmar\)) and [Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Myanmar\)) and certain junta conglomerates.[^cite_note-509] On March 22, 2021, Secretary of State Antony Blinken announced sanctions on several military generals in response to a violent crackdown on peaceful protests.[^cite_note-510]

### Afghanistan

Main article: [2020–2021 U.S. troop withdrawal from Afghanistan](66883576.md)



Biden meeting with Afghan president [Ashraf Ghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Ghani) and chairman [Abdullah Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Abdullah), June 2021

President Biden delivers remarks on Afghanistan ([transcript](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Remarks%20by%20President%20Biden%20on%20the%20Way%20Forward%20in%20Afghanistan)).

In February 2020, the Trump administration [made a deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal) with the [Taliban](30635.md) to completely withdraw U.S. forces by May 1, 2021.[^cite_note-images-511] In April 2021, President Biden formally announced that American troops would instead withdraw from Afghanistan by September 11, 2021, which would signal an end to the U.S.'s longest war.[^cite_note-512] According to Princeton professor [Julian E. Zelizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_E._Zelizer), Biden "clearly learned a great deal from his time in the Obama presidency", and demonstrated that "he is a politician capable of learning and evolving, contrary to some of the skeptics in the primaries who thought he didn't understand how politics had changed." According to *Washington Post* journalist Steven Levingston, "Obama listened to military leaders who advised him that withdrawal would be a mistake. Biden, meanwhile, was the top administration official arguing for a much more limited role for American forces in Afghanistan. Later, Biden would go on to say that he could tell by Obama's 'body language' that he agreed with that assessment — even though he ultimately rejected it."[^cite_note-Nazaryan_2021-513]
