Soon after the withdrawal of U.S. troops started, the [Taliban](30635.md) launched an [offensive against the Afghan government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive), quickly advancing in front of a collapsing [Afghan Armed Forces](57836785.md).[^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] President Biden defended the withdrawal, saying "I trust the capacity of the Afghan military, who is better trained, better equipped and ... more competent in terms of conducting war."[^cite_note-516]

By early July 2021, most of the American troops in Afghanistan were withdrawn.[^cite_note-images-511] Biden addressed the withdrawal, stating that: "The likelihood there's going to be the Taliban overrunning everything and owning the whole country is highly unlikely."[^cite_note-images-511] On August 15, amid [an offensive by the Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive), the Afghan government collapsed, Afghan president [Ashraf Ghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Ghani) fled the country and [Kabul fell to the Taliban](68481047.md).[^cite_note-images-511][^cite_note-messy-517] Biden reacted by ordering 6,000 American troops to assist in the evacuation of American personnel and Afghan allies.[^cite_note-518] He has been criticized over the manner of the American withdrawal.[^cite_note-messy-517]

On August 16, Biden addressed the "messy" situation, taking responsibility for it ("the buck stops with me"), and admitting that the situation "unfolded more quickly than we had anticipated".[^cite_note-messy-517][^cite_note-buck1-519] He defended his decision to withdraw, saying that Americans should not be "dying in a war that Afghan forces are not willing to fight for themselves", since the "Afghan military collapsed \[against the Taliban], sometimes without trying to fight".[^cite_note-buck1-519][^cite_note-buck2-520] Biden partly attributed the lack of early evacuation of Afghan civilians to the Afghan government's opposition of a "mass exodus" which they thought would cause a "crisis of confidence".[^cite_note-buck2-520]



President Biden discussing the [fall of Kabul](68481047.md) with the [National Security Council](44026.md), August 2021

On August 26, a [suicide attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Kabul_airport_attack) was carried out by the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant - Khorasan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant_%E2%80%93_Khorasan_Province) at the [Hamid Karzai International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai_International_Airport), killing more than 170 people, including at least 62 Afghan civilians, 13 US service members, two British nationals and the child of a third British national.[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522] Biden made a public address after the attack, in which he honored the American service members who were killed, calling them "heroes" and saying they lost their lives "in the service of liberty", and stated that the US had evacuated more than 100,000 Americans, Afghans, and others. He expressed deep sorrow for the Afghan victims as well. Biden said to those who wished harm upon the US that "we will hunt you down and make you pay."[^cite_note-523][^cite_note-524] Biden received increasingly harsh criticism from both Republicans and Democrats in the US Congress, with Republicans calling for his resignation or for his impeachment.[^cite_note-525][^cite_note-526][^cite_note-527]

After the Taliban takeover of Afghanistan, the Biden administration [froze about \$9 billion in assets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_frozen_assets) belonging to the [Afghan central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank), blocking the Taliban from accessing these billions of dollars in [reserves](1015869.md) held in U.S. bank accounts.[^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529]

In February 2022, Biden signed an executive order that seeks to unfreeze approximately \$3.5 billion of Afghan assets in the U.S. for the purpose of humanitarian assistance in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-530]

On July 31, 2022, [Al-Qaida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaida) leader [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri) was killed in Kabul by [an American drone strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Ayman_al-Zawahiri) approved by Biden.[^cite_note-531]

### Iran

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93United_States_relations_during_the_Biden_administration)

Relations with Iran during the Biden administration centered on attempts to revive the 2015 nuclear agreement amid sanctions, cyberattacks, and proxy conflicts. U.S. officials sought a return to the [JCPOA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JCPOA),[^cite_note-blunder-532] but Vienna talks stalled,[^cite_note-1953-2023_timeline-533][^cite_note-stalling-534] as [Iran increased uranium enrichment](721807.md) and imposed retaliatory sanctions,[^cite_note-1953-2023_timeline-533][^cite_note-15sanct-535] while the U.S. imposed new sanctions over missile programs, oil exports, and human rights abuses.[^cite_note-Blinkdeal-536][^cite_note-Moscsanct-537][^cite_note-IranChina-538]

Proxy attacks on U.S. bases continued,[^cite_note-Kataib-539] intensifying after the Gaza war in late 2023,[^cite_note-proiran-540] and were met with American strikes.[^cite_note-65milit-541] The period also saw disputes over the [assassination of Qasem Soleimani](62726668.md),[^cite_note-Motamedi-542][^cite_note-avengeUS-543][^cite_note-offercon-544] and military escalations across the Gulf region.[^cite_note-ErbilUS-545][^cite_note-Iranseizes-546][^cite_note-1000US-547] In 2023, a breakthrough occurred with a U.S.–Iran prisoner swap and the release of frozen Iranian funds,[^cite_note-6Bdeal-548][^cite_note-humani-549][^cite_note-breakth-550] though indirect diplomacy remained fragile.[^cite_note-EUhoped-551] Later accusations of Iranian cyber interference in the 2024 U.S. election,[^cite_note-Iranhack-552][^cite_note-Iranmail-553][^cite_note-AI3lines-554] along with alleged assassination plots in the U.S.,[^cite_note-KD-555] further strained relations. By late 2024, relations remained tense and adversarial.

### Saudi Arabia and Yemen



Secretary of Defense [Lloyd Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Austin) and Saudi Arabia's deputy minister of defense Prince [Khalid bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalid_bin_Salman_bin_Abdulaziz_Al_Saud), July 2021



President [Joe Biden](145422.md) and Saudi crown prince [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md) bumps fist at Al-Salam Palace in [Jeddah](277718.md), in July 2022.

[^cite_note-556]

Biden ordered a halt in the [arms sales to Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_arms_deal) and the United Arab Emirates which the Trump administration had previously agreed to.[^cite_note-557] Two years after [Jamal Khashoggi's assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Jamal_Khashoggi), Avril Haines, the [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md) under Biden's administration, announced that the intelligence report into the case against the Saudi government would be declassified. It was reported that Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md) would be blamed for the murder, as was concluded by the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA).[^cite_note-558]

On February 4, 2021, the Biden administration announced that the U.S. was ending [its support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._support_for_Saudi-led_operations_in_Yemen) for the [Saudi-led bombing campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen) in [Yemen](350939.md). President Biden in his first visit to the State Department as president said "this war has to end" and that the conflict has created "a humanitarian and strategic catastrophe".[^cite_note-559] However, the details of the end of American involvement in the war have yet to be released as of April 2021.[^cite_note-560]

In September 2021, Biden's national security adviser [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan) met in Saudi Arabia with Crown Prince Bin Salman to discuss the [high oil prices](5137675.md).[^cite_note-561] The [record-high energy prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_global_energy_crisis) were driven by a global surge in demand as the world quit the economic recession caused by COVID-19.[^cite_note-562][^cite_note-563] The Biden administration was pressed on potential oil deals with Saudi Arabia, [Venezuela](32374.md), and [Iran](14653.md) that would have them increase their [oil production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_production).[^cite_note-564][^cite_note-565] However, so far, Saudi Arabia and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) have declined requests from the US.[^cite_note-566][^cite_note-567]

As a presidential candidate, Joe Biden had vowed to make the Saudis "pay the price" and make them a "pariah" state, citing the Kingdom's involvement in the [assassination of Jamal Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Jamal_Khashoggi).[^cite_note-568] But, in June 2022, the White House confirmed that Biden was to visit Saudi Arabia and meet Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md), during his [Middle East](19323.md) trip in July.[^cite_note-F24-15-06-2022-569][^cite_note-570] The announcement came after [inflation in the US rose to a 40-year high](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021-2022_inflation_surge#North_America).[^cite_note-571] The [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)) also had an impact on the oil and gas prices in the US. Biden repeatedly appealed to the Saudis to increase oil production, but the Kingdom turned down such requests.[^cite_note-WP-03-06-2022-572] Biden's planned visit was seen as a move to seek Saudi assistance to ease the oil and gas prices at home.[^cite_note-F24-15-06-2022-569][^cite_note-573] However, [human rights activists](4358082.md) and Democratic lawmakers warned Biden that the visit could send signals to Saudi that their horrific human rights violations could be exempted.[^cite_note-F24-15-06-2022-569] Saudi [dissidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissidents) living in the US said that as Saudi activists who were wronged by Prince Mohammed, they "feel betrayed by Biden".[^cite_note-WP-03-06-2022-572] Son of [Saad Aljabri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saad_Aljabri), Khalid AlJabri said Biden's meeting with MbS would be "equivalent of a presidential pardon for murder".[^cite_note-574] Democratic representative [Adam Schiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Schiff) also criticized the visit saying, "I wouldn't go. I wouldn't shake his hand. This is someone who butchered an American resident, cut him up into pieces and in the most terrible and premeditated way."[^cite_note-575]



Secretary of State [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) and foreign ministers of the [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md) member states in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, April 19, 2024

On July 10, 2022, president Biden defended his trip to Saudi Arabia, saying humans rights were on his agenda.[^cite_note-576] In an [op-ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op-ed), he wrote that he aims to "reorient and not rupture relations with a country that's been a strategic partner for 80 years", and that Saudi Arabia has helped to restore unity among the six countries of [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md) and has fully supported the truce in the context of the [Yemen war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Civil_War_\(2014%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-577]

Ahead of Biden's visit to Saudi Arabia, reports revealed that the Biden administration could possibly lift a ban on sales of [offensive weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offensive_weapons) to Riyadh. However, U.S. national security adviser [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan) they were focused on a "real ceasefire", and on Saudi efforts to end the war.[^cite_note-578] After the meeting with Saudi officials, Biden announced that the Kingdom committed to extend the [truce in Yemen](34801006.md).[^cite_note-579] On August 2, 2022, the State Department approved the potential sale of 300 MIM-104E Guidance Enhanced Missile-Tactical Ballistic Missiles (GEM-T) for the [Patriot missile defense system](52024.md) to Saudi Arabia. It also approved support equipment, spares and technical support to the Arab nation. In addition, the State Department also approved the potential sale of 96 [Terminal High Altitude Area Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_High_Altitude_Area_Defense) (THAAD) missile defense system interceptors and support equipment to the UAE.[^cite_note-580][^cite_note-581] However, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) said the US should suspend sales of both offensive and defensive arms to Saudi Arabia and the UAE, which have used American weapons in unlawful airstrikes. HRW said a policy reversal by the US could lead to added rights violations in Yemen.[^cite_note-582]

### United Arab Emirates



Secretary Blinken, Israeli foreign minister [Yair Lapid](2448072.md) and UAE foreign minister [Abdullah bin Zayed Al Nahyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_bin_Zayed_Al_Nahyan) on October 13, 2021

The U.S. authorities indicted [Thomas J. Barrack Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_J._Barrack_Jr.), an outside adviser to Donald Trump during and after the [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) campaign. They alleged he acted as an unregistered foreign lobbyist for the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md). Barrack was also accused of obstruction of justice by giving false statements to the investigators.[^cite_note-583][^cite_note-584] He was found not guilty on all charges in November 2022.[^cite_note-585] The [DOJ](52563.md) also prosecuted some men for funneling more than \$3.5 million to [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) from [George Nader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Nader_\(businessman\)), the royal adviser of the UAE.[^cite_note-586]

While federal prosecutors accused the Emirates of interfering in American politics from both sides, the relations with the Arab nation during Biden's presidency didn't witness much of the expected changes. The UAE was seen escaping its blunder-filled history of relations with the US, despite Biden's repetitive criticism against the Emirates' [human rights violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations) and its attempts of infiltrating the US politics.[^cite_note-587] Moreover, the Biden administration also permitted the arms sales of \$23 billion to the UAE, which was initiated by Donald Trump and involved a transfer of sophisticated weaponry like the [F-35](11812.md) fighter jets.[^cite_note-588] The US Justice Department did not charge any Emirati in the case. However, Barrack's indictment identified three UAE officials who were hosts at his reception in the Gulf nation after Trump's 2016 elections, and two others who were involved. Amongst the hosts was Abu Dhabi Crown Prince [Mohamed bin Zayed](2121945.md), the UAE's national security adviser [Tahnoun bin Zayed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahnoun_bin_Zayed_Al_Nahyan_\(national_security_advisor\)) and director of the Emirati intelligence service, Ali Mohammed Hammad Al Shamsi. The fourth Emirati official was Abdullah Khalifa Al Ghafli, who "tasked" Barrack to push Emirati interests with America. Another official was [Yousef Al Otaiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yousef_Al_Otaiba), who asked to remain anonymous in discussions over private matters.[^cite_note-589]

On September 23, 2024, the UAE president Mohamed bin Zayed visited the White House to hold meetings with President Biden and Kamala Harris. It marked the first ever meeting of an Emirati President to Washington. Biden and Harris met Mohamed separately, focusing on future bilateral economic and technological relations. Prior to the meeting, Biden received a letter from the lawmakers to raise concerns with the UAE around its military support to the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md) (RSF) in Sudan. Appreciating Biden's efforts towards the [Sudan crisis](73556699.md), the lawmakers stated that the Emirati actions could become an obstruction. They urged Biden to address the concerns to Mohammed and press the UAE to cease the support to RSF.[^cite_note-590][^cite_note-591][^cite_note-592]

### Israel



President Biden meets [Israeli Prime Minister](24898.md) [Yair Lapid](2448072.md) and [Israeli President](153049.md) [Isaac Herzog](4645588.md) on his visit to Israel, July 13, 2022.

Early on, the Biden administration addressed Trump's [recognition of Jerusalem as the Israeli capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_recognition_of_Jerusalem_as_capital_of_Israel). The White House confirmed that the [U.S. Embassy](57417950.md) would remain in [Jerusalem](16043.md) and it would continue to be recognized as the capital. The administration also expressed support for the [Abraham Accords](64883396.md) while wanting to expand on them, although it shied away from using that name, instead referring to it simply as "the normalization process".[^cite_note-593][^cite_note-594][^cite_note-595]

On May 13, 2021, in the aftermath of the [Al-Aqsa mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibli_Mosque) conflict, the Biden administration was accused of being indifferent towards the violent conflict between Israeli statehood and the Palestinian minority there. Critics on both sides identified the reaction by the White House as "lame and late".[^cite_note-596]

On May 21, 2021, a ceasefire was brokered between [Israel](9282173.md) and [Hamas](13913.md) after [eleven days of clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis). According to Biden, the US will be playing a key role to rebuild damaged infrastructure in the Gaza alongside the [Palestinian authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_authority).[^cite_note-597][^cite_note-598]

#### Gaza War

Further information: [Gaza war](74998519.md) and [United States support for Israel in the Gaza war](65284610.md)

President Biden delivers remarks on the Hamas terrorist attacks in Israel.



Biden and Israeli prime minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md), October 18, 2023

After [the October 7 attack](74998317.md) in Israel, Biden stated, "We stand with Israel" three days later and emphasized the US's role in potentially freeing American hostages in Gaza.[^cite_note-599]

On October 14, Biden condemned the [murder of Wadea Al-Fayoume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Wadea_Al-Fayoume), a six-year-old Muslim boy in Chicago, by the boy's landlord. The murder was an alleged hate crime inspired by the conflict. Biden said, "There is no place in America for hate against anyone."[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-601]

During a speech at the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign) Dinner on October 15, a protestor chanted "let Gaza live" and "ceasefire now" to which Biden responded that he could not hear the protestor, but then later said "I get it. I'm not sure that's a good thing. No, I'm only joking."[^cite_note-602][^cite_note-603]

Biden's trip to Jordan to meet [King Abdullah II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_II_of_Jordan), [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md), and [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md) was "mutually" canceled on October 17 and indefinitely postponed.[^cite_note-604][^cite_note-605][^cite_note-606]

President Biden traveled to [Tel Aviv](31453.md), Israel on October 18 and met with Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md), resulting in [humanitarian aid](434679.md) being allowed into Gaza, including food, water, and medicine.[^cite_note-607] During the trip, Biden blamed the [al-Ahli Arab Hospital explosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ahli_Arab_Hospital_explosion) on "an errant rocket fired by a terrorist group in Gaza," citing US intelligence reports.[^cite_note-608][^cite_note-609] He also promised to ask congress for an "unprecedented support package for Israel's defense," potentially tied to a bill with support for Ukraine, Taiwan, and the [Southern Border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border).[^cite_note-610] Biden called on Congress to pass \$14.3 billion in emergency [military aid to Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93United_States_military_relations).[^cite_note-611] Israel already receives \$3.8 billion a year in military aid.[^cite_note-612] Biden also announced that the US would send \$100 million in humanitarian aid to Gaza and the West Bank.[^cite_note-613]

On November 1, after Jessica Rosenberg, a [rabbi](51273.md) and activist from [Jewish Voice for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Voice_for_Peace), heckled Biden during a speech in [Northfield, Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northfield,_Minnesota) calling for a ceasefire, Biden said, "I think we need a pause. A pause means give time to get the prisoners out."[^cite_note-614][^cite_note-615]

Starting November 9, Biden negotiated four-hour pauses for civilians to flee every day after his push for three-day pauses failed.[^cite_note-616] Starting on November 23, Israel agreed to a [four-day ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Gaza_war_ceasefire) for north of Gaza for six hours a day and a complete ceasefire south of Gaza in exchange for [hostages taken by Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Gaza_war_hostage_crisis?action=edit\&redlink=1). The deal was brokered by Qatar, Egypt, and the United States.[^cite_note-617][^cite_note-618][^cite_note-619]



Secretary of Defense [Lloyd Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Austin), Israeli Defense Minister [Yoav Gallant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoav_Gallant), Joint Chiefs of Staff Chairman [Charles Q. Brown Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Q._Brown_Jr.) and Israeli Chief of General Staff [Herzi Halevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herzi_Halevi) in Tel Aviv, Israel, December 18, 2023

[Ian Bremmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Bremmer), an American political scientist, stated the Biden administration's position unconditionally supporting Israel had left him as isolated on the world stage as Russia's president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-620] Biden has reportedly expressed frustration with Netanyahu's handling of the war, although this has not led to a major shift in American policy.[^cite_note-nbcnews_Feb-621][^cite_note-622]

Congressional oversight on arms sales has been sidestepped by the Biden administration on two occasions in December 2023.[^cite_note-623] Usually the [Arms Export Control Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_Export_Control_Act) would require the [State Department](31975.md), on behalf of the president, to provide [U.S. Congress](31756.md) advance notification of government-to-government foreign military sales of defense equipment. But the secretary of state [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) certified the existence of an "emergency" and therefore the requirement was lifted twice which raised objections from lawmakers from the [Democratic Party](5043544.md).[^cite_note-624]



[Abdullah II of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_II_of_Jordan) and [Crown Prince Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein,_Crown_Prince_of_Jordan) with President Biden in February 2024

In February 2024, the Biden administration warned the government of Israel against its announced advance against [Rafah](584373.md),[^cite_note-625] and [NBC news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_news) reported that Biden "has been venting his frustration in recent private conversations, some of them with campaign donors, over his inability to persuade Israel to change its military tactics in the Gaza Strip".[^cite_note-nbcnews_Feb-621] On February 16, 2024, *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported that the administration is pushing for a cease-fire deal but on the other hand is preparing another delivery of bombs and other weapons and that the administration has to notify "congressional committee leaders who would need to approve the transfer."[^cite_note-626] On February 20, the U.S. government [vetoed a U.N. Security Council resolution](1900272.md) demanding a cease-fire in Gaza.[^cite_note-627]

After the [killing of Palestinian civilians receiving food aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Rashid_humanitarian_aid_incident) on February 29, Biden expressed that the current level of aid flowing into Gaza was insufficient.[^cite_note-628] On March 3, US military began airdropping food aid into Gaza.[^cite_note-629] Some experts called the US airdrops performative and claimed they would do little to alleviate the [food situation in Gaza](75828824.md).[^cite_note-630] In July 2024, following Biden's decision not to seek re-election, Palestinian advocacy groups stated they believed that the war would define Biden's presidential legacy.[^cite_note-631]



Israeli Prime Minister Netanyahu with US ambassador to Israel [Jack Lew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Lew) (left) and US Secretary of State [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken), August 19, 2024



President Biden and Israeli President [Isaac Herzog](4645588.md), November 12, 2024

On March 30, 2024, the Biden administration authorized \$2.5 billion in weapons transfers to Israel.[^cite_note-632] On April 24, 2024, Biden signed a [\$95 billion security package](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Law_118-50) which included around \$17 billion in military aid for Israel.[^cite_note-633] In June 2024, the United States officially signed a Letter of Offer and Acceptance, allowing Israel to [purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_Israeli_procurement) 25 additional [Lockheed Martin F-35](11812.md) stealth fighter jets for \$3 billion.[^cite_note-634]

On July 10, 2024, the Biden administration resumed shipments of the [500-pound bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_82_bomb) to Israel, which were halted in May over concerns about the [humanitarian impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)) of Israel's use of them in [killing Palestinians in Gaza](75130785.md).[^cite_note-635] On August 9, 2024, the Department of State said the United States would send Israel an additional \$3.5 billion to spend on US-made weapons and military equipment.[^cite_note-636] On August 13, 2024, the Department of State announced that the U.S. had approved a \$20 billion weapons package sale to Israel, which included fighter jets and advanced air-to-air missiles.[^cite_note-637]

On September 17, 2024, thousands of handheld [pagers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pager) used by [Hezbollah](13919.md) simultaneously [exploded across Lebanon and Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Lebanon_pager_explosions).[^cite_note-638] The attack came just a day after the Biden administration's special envoy [Amos Hochstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amos_Hochstein) visited Israel and warned Benjamin Netanyahu against provoking a major [escalation](75895273.md) in Lebanon.[^cite_note-639]

On November 20, 2024, a UN Security Council draft resolution that demanded an immediate and unconditional ceasefire in the Gaza Strip was [vetoed by the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_vetoed_United_Nations_Security_Council_resolutions) whereas all other members voted in favor. The draft resolution would have rejected "any effort to starve Palestinians" and demanded the facilitation of full, rapid, safe and unhindered entry of aid at scale to and throughout the Strip and its delivery to all those in need.[^cite_note-640] On January 15, 2025, a [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) proposal backed by Biden was adopted just days before his leaving office.[^cite_note-641][^cite_note-642]

### Palestine



Biden with Palestinian president [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md) at the Palestinian presidential palace in Bethlehem, [West Bank](33209.md), July 15, 2022

During a July 2022 visit to Israel, Biden stressed the importance of keeping the [two-state vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution) alive. He met with [Palestinian National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Authority) President [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md) and announced a new aid package to the Palestinians.[^cite_note-Eglash-2022-643] During the administration of his predecessor Donald Trump, U.S. contributions to the [United Nations Relief and Works Agency for Palestine Refugees](32270.md) had been defunded.[^cite_note-644] On the Israeli end, Prime Minister Yair Lapid reaffirmed his position on a two-state solution.[^cite_note-Eglash-2022-643]

### Armenian genocide

On April 24, 2021, the Biden administration declared that the Turkish killings of Armenians at the start of the 20th century were a [genocide](12441.md). He is the first U.S. president to ever officially recognize the [Armenian genocide](140376.md).[^cite_note-645]

### Russia



Biden and Russian president Vladimir Putin at the [2021 Russia–United States summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Russia%E2%80%93United_States_summit) in Geneva, Switzerland

On the day of Biden's inauguration, the [Russian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_government) urged the new U.S. administration to take a "more constructive" approach in talks over the extension of the 2010 [New START](26709878.md) treaty, the sole remaining agreement limiting the number of U.S. and Russian [long-range nuclear warheads](400821.md).[^cite_note-646] In Biden's first telephone call as president with [Russian President](239646.md) [Vladimir Putin](32817.md), on January 26, 2021, Biden and Putin agreed to extend the New START treaty (which was set to expire in February 2021) by an additional five years.[^cite_note-647]

Biden and his administration condemned human rights violations by the Russian authorities, calling for the release of detained dissident and anti-corruption activist [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md), his wife, and the thousands of Russians who had [demonstrated in his support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Russian_protests); the U.S. called for the unconditional release of Navalny and the protestors and a credible investigation into [Navalny's poisoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poisoning_of_Alexei_Navalny).[^cite_note-648][^cite_note-649][^cite_note-650] On March 2, 2021, the U.S. and European Union imposed coordinated additional sanctions on Russian officials, as well as the [FSB](385990.md) and [GRU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GRU_\(G.U.\)), over Navalny's poisoning and imprisonment. The State Department also expanded existing sanctions from the Chemical and Biological Weapons Control and Warfare Elimination Act that had been imposed after the poisoning of Skripal.[^cite_note-Hansler-651] The Biden administration also planned to impose sanctions against Russia because of the [2020 SolarWinds cyberespionage campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_federal_government_data_breach), which compromised the computer systems of nine federal agencies.[^cite_note-PreparingtoSanction-652] Biden's national security adviser [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan) said the response "will include a mix of tools seen and unseen, and it will not simply be sanctions."[^cite_note-PreparingtoSanction-652][^cite_note-Hansler-651]

The Biden administration's comprehensive review into Russian activities has included an examination of [reports that the Russian government offered bounties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_bounty_program) to kill U.S. troops in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-653][^cite_note-654] The Biden administration said intelligence community has only "low to moderate" confidence in reports due to the fact that the bounty reports originated from "detainee reporting and because of the difficult operating environment in Afghanistan."[^cite_note-655][^cite_note-656] Biden called Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) a "killer" in an ABC News interview, and said that Russia would pay a price for election meddling.[^cite_note-657]

President Biden delivers remarks on Russia's invasion of Ukraine.

On May 19, 2021, the Biden administration lifted [CAATSA sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countering_America's_Adversaries_Through_Sanctions_Act) on the [Nord Stream 2](50335419.md) pipeline project between Russia and Germany. Despite Biden's personal opposition to the project, the U.S. State Department says that it concluded that it was in the "U.S. national interest" to waive the sanctions.[^cite_note-NordStream2-658] Russian deputy foreign minister [Sergei Ryabkov](31914796.md) welcomed the move as "a chance for a gradual transition toward the normalization of our bilateral ties."[^cite_note-NordStream2-658]



Biden visits [Ukrainian refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Ukrainian_refugee_crisis) in Warsaw, Poland, March 2022.

On June 16, 2021, [Biden met with Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Russia%E2%80%93United_States_summit) in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva), Switzerland. The two presidents discussed a number of topics, including stable policy on climate change, nuclear proliferation, and cybersecurity. Russia's activities regarding [Ukraine](31750.md), [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md), [Belarus](3457.md), and nationals jailed in each other's countries. The summit was significantly shorter than expected, only lasting three and a half of the predicted five hours.[^cite_note-659] Putin praised Biden as a knowledgeable and shrewd negotiator the next day.[^cite_note-660][^cite_note-661]

In November 2021, Putin stated that an [expansion of NATO's presence in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_NATO), especially the deployment of any [long-range missiles](38025.md) capable of striking Russian cities or [missile defense systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_national_missile_defense) similar to those in Romania and Poland, would be a "red line" issue for Russia.[^cite_note-662][^cite_note-663] In December 2021, Putin asked President Joe Biden for legal guarantees that NATO wouldn't expand eastward or put "weapons systems that threaten us in close vicinity to Russian territory."[^cite_note-664] The U.S. and NATO have rejected Putin's demands.[^cite_note-665][^cite_note-666]

On February 24, 2022, Biden condemned [Russia's invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia's_invasion_of_Ukraine), saying Putin "chose this war" and calling him "the aggressor". He announced new sanctions against Russia.[^cite_note-667] On February 25, the White House announced the US would personally sanction Putin and foreign minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md).[^cite_note-668] On February 28, the Biden administration announced sanctions against [Russia's central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Russia), prohibiting Americans from doing business with the bank and freezing the bank's assets.[^cite_note-669] On March 29, 2022, Biden appeared to heighten his condemnation concerning Putin made at the end of his NATO trip to Europe, stating that he makes "no apologies" for previously stating that "Putin cannot remain in power".[^cite_note-670] On April 12, he called the war a "genocide".[^cite_note-671] Biden criticized the Kremlin for "idle comments" on the possible use of [nuclear weapons](21785.md).[^cite_note-672]



Biden with his National Security Adviser [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan), February 19, 2023

On April 28, Biden asked Congress for an additional \$33 billion to assist Ukraine, including \$20 billion to provide weapons to Ukraine.[^cite_note-673] On May 10, the [House](19468510.md) passed legislation that would provide \$40 billion in new aid to Ukraine.[^cite_note-674] The *New York Times* reported that the United States provided real-time battlefield [targeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targeting_\(warfare\)) [intelligence](146678.md) to Ukraine that helped Ukrainian forces kill Russian generals and sink the Russian warship *[Moskva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_cruiser_Moskva)*.[^cite_note-675][^cite_note-676] The Biden administration has pledged to help the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) to prosecute Putin and others for war crimes committed during the invasion of Ukraine.[^cite_note-677][^cite_note-678]

On September 27, 2022, White House press secretary [Karine Jean-Pierre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karine_Jean-Pierre) encouraged Russian men fleeing their home country to avoid being drafted to apply for [asylum in the United States](7195971.md).[^cite_note-679] In early 2023, the Biden administration resumed deportations of Russians who [had fled Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_emigration_following_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) because of [mobilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Russian_mobilization) and [political persecution](70646883.md).[^cite_note-680]

In January 2024, the Biden administration rejected Putin's proposal for a [ceasefire in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_negotiations_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine). Biden's National Security Advisor [Jake Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Sullivan) informed Putin's foreign policy adviser [Yuri Ushakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Ushakov) that the United States would not discuss a ceasefire without Ukraine's participation.[^cite_note-681][^cite_note-682]

On May 30, 2024, Biden gave Ukraine permission to [strike targets inside Russia](70539619.md) using American-supplied weapons.[^cite_note-683] Biden said "They are authorized to be used in proximity to the border. We are not authorizing strikes 200 miles into Russia."[^cite_note-684] On August 9, 2024, a convoy of Russian troops in the [Kursk Oblast](480916.md) of Russia was [destroyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2024_Kursk_Oblast_incursion) in a strike by U.S.-supplied [HIMARS](147011.md) rocket system in what Russian milbloggers described as one of the bloodiest attacks of the entire war.[^cite_note-685][^cite_note-686]

### Ukraine

Further information: [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) and [United States and the Russian invasion of Ukraine](70376708.md)



Biden with Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) in Kyiv, Ukraine, February 20, 2023

In February 2022, amid [rising tensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) between Ukraine and Russia, the Biden administration faced questions for its year-long failure to nominate an ambassador to Ukraine.[^cite_note-687]

The [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) instigated significant and substantial support to Ukraine during the Biden presidency including two dozen military aid packages to assist them against the invasion. On October 28, 2022 [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) announced the 24th presidential drawdown of materiel worth \$275 million; the security assistance has totaled \$18.5 billion to Ukraine since January 2021.[^cite_note-stripes28Oct22-688] The aid includes 500 Excalibur precision-guided 155mm artillery rounds, 2000 155mm remote anti-armor mine systems, more than 1,300 anti-armor weapons, more than 2.75 million rounds of small arms ammunition, more HIMARS rockets, 125 [Humvees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMMWV), and four satellite communications antennas for Ukraine's [command and control](7092305.md) systems, as well as training for operation of the [NASAMS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASAMS) units.[^cite_note-nasamsImminent-689] Two NASAMS units arrived in Ukraine on November 7, 2022.[^cite_note-stripes28Oct22-688][^cite_note-2nasams-690][^cite_note-nasams16Nov22-694]

On February 20, 2023, four days before the one-year anniversary of the start of Russia's invasion of Ukraine, Biden [visited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_visit_by_Joe_Biden_to_Ukraine) Kyiv and met with President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and [First Lady of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Lady_of_Ukraine) [Olena Zelenska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olena_Zelenska).[^cite_note-695] While there, Biden affirmed more military aid to Ukraine and denounced the war.[^cite_note-696] The trip was unannounced, and involved major security coordinations to ensure safety.[^cite_note-697]

In 2022, Congress approved more than \$112 billion in aid to Ukraine. In October 2023, the Biden administration requested \$61.4 billion more in aid for Ukraine for the year ahead.[^cite_note-698]

### Europe



President Biden with European leaders at the [G20 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_G20_Rome_summit) in Rome, Italy, October 30, 2021



President Biden with European leaders in Berlin, Germany, October 18, 2024

President Biden and French President Macron stand in front of a villa in Rome, where they met each other to discuss the nuclear deal between Australia, the US and the UK.

President Biden and French President Macron meeting in Rome, October 29, 2021



President Biden with [European Commission President](24468.md) [Ursula von der Leyen](2991932.md) at a special meeting with [NATO](21133.md) in [Brussels](3708.md), Belgium, March 24, 2022



President Biden with [NATO Secretary General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Secretary_General) [Jens Stoltenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Stoltenberg) at the [2022 NATO Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Madrid_summit) in [Madrid](41188263.md), Spain, June 29, 2022

President Biden promised to repair "strained" relationships with European allies in contrast to his predecessor Trump. "An attack on one is an attack on all. That is our unshakeable vow," Biden said, referring to [Article 5 of the North Atlantic Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_5_of_the_North_Atlantic_Treaty) (the mutual defense clause).[^cite_note-699] Biden pledged support for the European project and for Ukraine's sovereignty as well as the need for global cooperation on fighting the pandemic and climate change.[^cite_note-700]

[U.S. relations with France](686250.md) deteriorated in September 2021 due to fallout from the [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS) security pact between the United States, the United Kingdom, and Australia, which aimed to counter Chinese power in the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md) region. As part of the agreement, the U.S. agreed to provide [nuclear-powered submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear-powered_submarine) to Australia. After entering into the agreement, the Australian government canceled an agreement that it had made with France for the [provision of French conventionally powered submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack-class_submarine). The deal angered the French government, which recalled its ambassador to the U.S. ([Philippe Étienne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_%C3%89tienne)) as well as the ambassador to Australia. Amid the diplomatic row, the French Foreign Ministry contended that it had been subjected to "duplicity, disdain and lies"[^cite_note-701][^cite_note-702] and French foreign minister [Jean-Yves Le Drian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Le_Drian) called the deal a "stab in the back".[^cite_note-703] In a conciliatory call a few days later, Biden and French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) agreed to reduce bilateral tensions, and the White House acknowledged the crisis could have been averted if there had been open consultations between allies.[^cite_note-704] A month later, Biden met Macron, telling him his administration was "clumsy" and that he was "under the impression that France had been informed long before" that France's deal with Australia was "not going through".[^cite_note-705]

In July 2024, the Biden administration announced its intention to deploy [long-range missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistic_missiles) in [Germany](11867.md) beginning in 2026. In response, Russian president Putin warned of a [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cold_War)-style missile crisis and threatened to deploy long-range missiles within striking distance of the West.[^cite_note-706][^cite_note-guardian-missiles-707] US weapons in Germany would include [SM-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RIM-174_Standard_ERAM) and [Tomahawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomahawk_\(missile_family\)) cruise missiles and [hypersonic weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-Range_Hypersonic_Weapon).[^cite_note-guardian-missiles-707] The United States' decision to deploy long-range missiles in Germany has been compared to the deployment of [Pershing II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pershing_II) launchers in Western Europe in 1979.[^cite_note-aljazeera-cold_war-708][^cite_note-guardian-missiles-707] Critics say the move would trigger a new [arms race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_race).[^cite_note-709] According to Russian military analysts, it would be extremely difficult to distinguish between a conventionally armed missile and a missile carrying a nuclear warhead, and Russia could respond by deploying longer-range nuclear systems targeting Germany.[^cite_note-710]

Biden has reiterated his commitment to maintaining peace in [Northern Ireland](21265.md) by resisting the possibility of a hard border as a result of [Brexit](41688778.md).[^cite_note-711]

### Cuba

The Biden administration has maintained the sanctions against [Cuba](5042481.md) that were issued by the [first Trump administration](52231341.md), despite one of Biden's campaign promises being to lift restrictions against the country.[^cite_note-712][^cite_note-\:20-713]

In June 2021, the Biden administration continued America's tradition of voting against an annual United Nations General Assembly resolution calling for an end to the [U.S. economic embargo against Cuba](1105954.md).[^cite_note-714] The resolution was adopted for the 29th time with 184 votes in favor, three abstentions, and two no votes: the U.S. and Israel.[^cite_note-715]

In July 2021, protesters gathered in front of the White House and demonstrators called on President Joe Biden to take action in Cuba.[^cite_note-716] The Biden administration sanctioned a key Cuban official and a government special forces unit known as the Boinas Negras for human rights abuses in the wake of historic protests on the island.[^cite_note-717] On July 22, 2021, directly before hosting a meeting with [Cuban American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Americans) leaders,[^cite_note-718] President Biden stated "I unequivocally condemn the mass detentions and sham trials that are unjustly sentencing to prison those who dared to speak out in an effort to intimidate and threaten the Cuban people into silence."[^cite_note-719] President Biden has also ordered government specialists to develop ideas for the U.S. to unilaterally extend internet access on the island, and he has promised to enhance backing for Cuban dissidents.[^cite_note-720]

In August 2021, Biden sanctioned three additional Cuban officials who were also reportedly involved in the suppression of anti-government protesters in Cuba.[^cite_note-721]

In December 2021, 114 Democratic House members signed a letter that urged President Biden to lift restrictions and sanctions against Cuba in order to make their access to food and medicine easier.[^cite_note-\:20-713]

In January 2022, Biden again sanctioned Cuba officials, this time placing travel restrictions on eight members of the Cuban government.[^cite_note-722]

In May 2022, the Biden administration lifted some of the sanctions, with policy changes such as expansion of flights to Cuba and resumption of a family reunification program. In January 2023, the Biden administration made changes to its immigration policy, to limit the amount of Cuban migrants entering the United States.

In the final week of his presidency, the Biden administration removed Cuba from the [state sponsors of terrorism list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Sponsors_of_Terrorism_\(U.S._list\)), in concert with a prisoner exchange, brokered by the Vatican.[^cite_note-723]

### Africa



President Biden meeting with [Kenyan President](392265.md) [Uhuru Kenyatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uhuru_Kenyatta), October 14, 2021



President Biden meeting with [South African President](321700.md) [Cyril Ramaphosa](1089194.md), September 16, 2022

Biden hosted a three-day U.S.-Africa summit in Washington in December 2022, attended by 49 African national leaders.[^cite_note-AllInAfrica-724] The meeting was the first such summit since 2014.[^cite_note-AllInAfrica-724][^cite_note-Walsh-725] The leaders of every [African nation in good standing](30486666.md) with the [African Union](10634933.md) (AU), except [Eritrea](17238590.md), were invited to the summit.[^cite_note-Walsh-725] The leaders of African nations not in good standing with the AU (mostly those who had come to power through [military coups](25914154.md)) were also not invited.[^cite_note-Walsh-725]

At the summit, Biden announced U.S. support for the AU joining the [G20](2206608.md) group of major economies, a long-sought goal for Africa.[^cite_note-726] The summit was part of a broader effort by the U.S. to rebuild U.S.-African relations and counter Chinese influence on the continent.[^cite_note-AllInAfrica-724] During the summit, the administration announced \$800 million in new deals with [Cisco Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisco_Systems) and Cybastion to combat cyberthreats targeting Africa, a bid to blunt the dominance of the Chinese firm [Huawei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei) in Africa.[^cite_note-Revitalize-727] The administration also signed a memorandum of understanding in support of the [African Continental Free Trade Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Continental_Free_Trade_Area) to reduce [trade barriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_barrier) in Africa,[^cite_note-Revitalize-727] and committed \$55 billion to Africa over the next three years, focused on preexisting U.S. initiatives,[^cite_note-Revitalize-727][^cite_note-AllInAfrica-724] such as the trade-focused Prosper Africa and [Africa Growth and Opportunity Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa_Growth_and_Opportunity_Act), as well as the [Power Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Africa) initiative, which aims to increase connections of Africans to the [electric grid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_grid).[^cite_note-AllInAfrica-724] The administration also emphasized initiatives in technology and cybersecurity, health, [clean energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_energy) and the environment, and other areas.[^cite_note-Revitalize-727][^cite_note-Walsh-725] Biden committed an additional \$2 billion for emergency humanitarian aid and \$11 billion for [food security](216361.md) programs in Africa.[^cite_note-Walsh-725] The administration also expanded ties with [West Africa](67393.md), including support for infrastructure improvements at the [Benin](3459.md) seaport of [Cotonou](56647.md), a key part of the West African economy.[^cite_note-AllInAfrica-724][^cite_note-728] Biden appointed a longtime U.S. diplomat, [Johnnie Carson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnnie_Carson), to coordinate implementation of U.S. actions following up the summit.[^cite_note-Gardner-729]

During the 2022 summit, Biden and senior administration officials also met privately with six African leaders facing elections in 2023, pushing them to ensure [free and fair elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_and_fair_elections) in their nations.[^cite_note-Gardner-729]

Senator [Bob Menendez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Menendez), the Democratic chairman of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, has criticized the Biden administration for hesitating to impose [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_sanctions) on the governments of [Sudan](27421.md) and [Ethiopia](187749.md), where many atrocities and [war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_in_the_Tigray_War) were committed in the [Tigray War](65760352.md).[^cite_note-730]

### Americans detained abroad



President Biden and Vice President Harris greet [Russian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Americans) journalist [Alsu Kurmasheva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsu_Kurmasheva) on August 1, 2024, after her release in a [prisoner swap with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Ankara_prisoner_exchange).

In July 2022, President Biden signed an executive order aimed at deterring the wrongful detention of Americans abroad.[^cite_note-731] According to an estimate by The James W. Foley Legacy Foundation, there are at least 67 [U.S. citizens who are currently imprisoned abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_American_people_imprisoned_abroad). The foundation further estimates that 90% of those are wrongly detained by foreign governments hostile to the U.S., including [Venezuela](32374.md), [Russia](25391.md), [China](5405.md), [Iran](14653.md), and others.[^cite_note-Guarino-2022-732] Dozens of families of Americans who are detained abroad say President Biden has failed to adequately address the crisis.[^cite_note-Guarino-2022-732] They formed a group called "[Bring Our Families Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bring_Our_Families_Home) Campaign" to pressure Biden to do more.[^cite_note-733]

In September 2023, President Biden reached a deal to release five prisoners held by Iran. In exchange, \$6 billion of frozen Iranian assets were released.[^cite_note-734] The U.S. said the unfrozen money would be held in restricted accounts so that it could only be spent on humanitarian goods such as food and medicine.[^cite_note-735]

In August 2024, President Biden [reached a deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Russian_prisoner_exchange) to secure the largest [prisoner exchange](17209653.md) since the [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-Washington_Post-2024-736] The exchange included the release of Americans [Evan Gershkovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Gershkovich), [Paul Whelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Whelan), and [Alsu Kurmasheva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsu_Kurmasheva); thirteen others from Russia and Belarus; and Russians held by the U.S., Slovenia, Norway, Poland, and Germany.[^cite_note-737] Turkey and Estonia were also involved in the deal.[^cite_note-Washington_Post-2024-736] Biden stressed the importance of alliances on the world stage by describing the achievement as "a powerful example of why it's vital to have friends in this world."[^cite_note-738]

### Worldwide LGBT rights

On February 4, 2021, Biden issued a presidential memorandum for expanding protection of [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_community) rights worldwide, which includes the possibility to impose financial sanctions.[^cite_note-739]

The [US State Department](31975.md) released a statement on [Intersex Awareness Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex_Awareness_Day) promoting LGBTQI+ rights around the globe, saying, "[Intersex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex) persons often face stigma and discrimination in accessing education, healthcare, and legal recognition, and are subjected to medically unnecessary [surgeries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex_medical_interventions). These harmful practices, which can cause lifelong negative physical and emotional consequences, are a medical form of so-called [conversion therapy](26410.md) practices in that they seek to physically "convert" Intersex children into non-Intersex children."[^cite_note-740]

Biden announced he would be removing [Uganda](31816.md) from the [African Growth and Opportunity Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Growth_and_Opportunity_Act) trade deal[^cite_note-741] over the country's [anti-LGBT bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Homosexuality_Act,_2023) that included the [death penalty](5902.md) for "aggravated homosexuality" and [life-in-prison](148681.md) for identifying as LGBT.[^cite_note-742][^cite_note-743]

## Investigations of Biden

### Hur special counsel investigation

Further information: [Joe Biden classified documents incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_classified_documents_incident)

Biden's attorneys informed the [National Archives Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_Administration) in November 2022 that [classified](252857.md) documents from before Biden's presidency had been found at the [Penn Biden Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_Biden_Center). Days later Attorney General Garland tasked U.S. Attorney [John R. Lausch Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Lausch_Jr.) with conducting an initial investigation.[^cite_note-whatweknow1-744][^cite_note-whatweknow2-745][^cite_note-CNN_timeline-746] On January 5, 2023, Lausch advised Garland that the assignment of a [special counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_counsel) was warranted.[^cite_note-whatweknow1-744][^cite_note-whatweknow2-745][^cite_note-CNN_timeline-746] On January 12, Garland announced that he was appointing a special counsel to investigate "possible unauthorized removal and retention of classified documents or other records",[^cite_note-747][^cite_note-748] and appointed [Robert K. Hur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_K._Hur) as special counsel.[^cite_note-749] Hur released his report on February 8, 2024, stating that the "evidence does not establish Mr. Biden's guilt beyond a reasonable doubt".[^cite_note-750] The report's final conclusion was that "no criminal charges are warranted in this matter" and that it would be "the same even if there was no policy against charging a sitting president".[^cite_note-751]

### Congressional investigations

See also: [United States House Oversight Committee investigation into the Biden family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Oversight_Committee_investigation_into_the_Biden_family), [United States House Judiciary Select Subcommittee on the Weaponization of the Federal Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Judiciary_Select_Subcommittee_on_the_Weaponization_of_the_Federal_Government), [Efforts to impeach Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efforts_to_impeach_Joe_Biden), and [Impeachment inquiry into Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_into_Joe_Biden)

On September 12, 2023, Speaker McCarthy acceded to the right wing of the House Republican Conference and announced the launch of an [impeachment inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_in_the_United_States) into Biden.[^cite_note-CNN0912a-752][^cite_note-753][^cite_note-754] McCarthy claimed that earlier findings of House investigations "paint a picture of [corruption](66220.md)" involving Biden and his relatives.[^cite_note-AP1-755] Prior investigations have failed to find evidence of wrongdoing by the president.[^cite_note-760] The impeachment inquiry fizzled out by April 2024.[^cite_note-761]

### 2022 midterm elections

Main article: [2022 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections)

Despite Biden's low approvals, a [red wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_elections_in_the_United_States) did not occur during the 2022 mid-term elections as many had anticipated. Democrats expanded their [narrow Senate majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections) while Republicans [took control of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections) by a far smaller margin than expected.[^cite_note-766] This was largely attributed to a backlash against [the Supreme Court's](31737.md) decision in *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)*,[^cite_note-767] as well as the [perceived extremeness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_extremism) of certain Republican candidates in competitive races.

It was the first midterm election since [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_United_States_gubernatorial_elections) in which the party of the incumbent president achieved a net gain in governorships, and the first since [1934](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1934_United_States_elections) in which the president's party didn't lose any state legislative chambers or incumbent senators.[^cite_note-768]

Many [pundits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pundits) had failed to predict the Democrats' resilient performance; [Simon Rosenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Rosenberg) was one exception.[^cite_note-769] Polls for the election cycle were the most accurate since [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_United_States_elections), though Republican-aligned pollsters such as the [Trafalgar Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trafalgar_Group) had a notable polling miss.[^cite_note-770][^cite_note-771]

The results drew praise from the Democratic Party,[^cite_note-772] and Biden celebrated the results as a strong day for democracy.[^cite_note-773]

Democratic overperformance in these elections are considered to have played a factor in the party's defeat in the 2024 elections, with its results misinterpreted as support for Biden's initial bid for re-election.[^cite_note-774][^cite_note-775]

After the election, Kevin McCarthy replaced Nancy Pelosi as Speaker of the House, while Hakeem Jeffries became the House Minority Leader.

### 2024 elections and transition period

#### Initial re-election campaign and withdrawal

Main articles: [Joe Biden 2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign), [2024 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), and [Withdrawal of Joe Biden from the 2024 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election)

President Biden's announcement that he would not run for re-election

After speculation Biden would not seek re-election due to his advanced age and poor job approval,[^cite_note-776] Biden officially announced [his reelection campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign) for the [Democratic nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) in the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md) on April 25, 2023.[^cite_note-777] [Representative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Representative) [Dean Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Phillips) ran against Biden in the primaries but was unsuccessful.[^cite_note-778][^cite_note-779] Phillips campaigned as a younger alternative to Biden, who would be a stronger opponent to Trump.[^cite_note-780][^cite_note-781] Biden became the presumptive Democratic party nominee on March 12, 2024, facing no effective challengers in the primaries.[^cite_note-782] Following what was widely viewed as a lackluster performance in the [first presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate) against presumptive Republican nominee Donald Trump and numerous [age and health concerns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_of_Joe_Biden), Biden faced [repeated calls to suspend his candidacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election). On July 21, 2024, Biden announced in a post on [X](9988187.md) (formerly known as Twitter) that he was suspending his campaign. He stated that "while it has been my intention to seek re-election, I believe it is in the best interest of my party and the country for me to stand down and to focus solely on fulfilling my duties as President for the remainder of my term", and immediately endorsed Vice President Kamala Harris to replace him in his place as the party's presidential nominee.[^cite_note-783]

#### General election and transition period

Main article: [2024 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_elections)

Further information: [Kamala Harris 2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris_2024_presidential_campaign), [Donald Trump 2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign), [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md), and [Second presidential transition of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_presidential_transition_of_Donald_Trump)



Republican [Donald Trump](4848272.md) defeated Democrat [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) in the 2024 presidential election.



Outgoing president Joe Biden and President-elect [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) on November 13, 2024

President Biden's farewell address on January 15, 2025

Following the [attempted assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Donald_Trump_in_Pennsylvania) of his then-opponent and then-presumptive Republican nominee former president Donald Trump, Biden condemned the shooting and, in an [Oval Office address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Oval_Office_Address) the next day, spoke about the "need for to lower the temperature" of its political rhetoric.[^cite_note-784]

Biden defended his record as president at the [Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_National_Convention) and advocated for the election of Harris in one of the last speeches of his presidency.[^cite_note-785] The convention was largely portrayed as an honoring of his political legacy.[^cite_note-786] In the general election, Harris was defeated by Trump. Some Democrats attributed Harris' loss to Biden's unpopularity on issues such as the economy and his refusal to withdraw from the race sooner.[^cite_note-787][^cite_note-legacyNeg1-32] In the concurrent [congressional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_elections), Republicans also retained a narrow majority in the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) and took control of the [Senate](24909346.md).

On January 15, 2025, Biden gave a [farewell address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden's_farewell_address) in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) days before the [second inauguration of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump).[^cite_note-788]

## Approval ratings and image

See also: [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration), [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration), [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration), and [2024–2025 opinion polling on the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration)

Very early in Biden's presidency, opinion polls found that Biden's approval ratings were steadier than Trump's, with an average approval rating of 55% and an average disapproval rate of 39%.[^cite_note-789] Biden's early approval ratings have been more polarized than Trump's, with 98% of Democrats, 61% of independents and 11% of Republicans approving of Biden's presidency in February 2021, a party gap of 87%.[^cite_note-790] Around the end of his first hundred days in office, Biden's approval rating was higher than Trump's but was the third worst since the presidency of [Harry Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman); only Trump and [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) scored lower.[^cite_note-791][^cite_note-792]

After the [fall of Kabul](68481047.md) and [the surge of COVID-19 cases](63136490.md#May_to_August_2021) due to the [Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant) in July and August 2021, Biden's approval rating began to steadily decline, from a high of 52.7% approval on July 26, 2021, to 45.9% approval by September 3, 2021, according to FiveThirtyEight.[^cite_note-793][^cite_note-794] While the White House emphasized COVID-19 as causing his low approval rating,[^cite_note-795] [inflation](38286.md), the [highest in nearly 40 years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_inflation_surge),[^cite_note-796][^cite_note-797] has also been described as a cause.[^cite_note-798][^cite_note-799] Biden's lowest approval rating on record comes from a [Quinnipiac University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinnipiac_University) poll in July 2022, which showed just 31% of respondents approving of his performance as president.[^cite_note-800]

By the one-year anniversary of Biden's presidency on January 20, 2022, [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) recorded the average approval rating for Biden's first year as 49%, which was the second-lowest first-year average approval rating for any American president since [World War II](32927.md); only Trump's first-year average of 38.4% was lower.[^cite_note-Hubbard-801][^cite_note-802] Gallup further noted that there was greater political polarization in Biden's approval ratings than any other first-year president in modern history, with 91% of Democrats supporting Biden while just 8% of Republicans supported him, resulting in a party gap of 83%. The only other year of any presidency that saw greater polarization was Trump's final year in office.[^cite_note-Hubbard-801][^cite_note-803] The July 2022 Gallup survey saw Biden's sixth quarter approval rating of 40%, the lowest sixth quarter rating of any president in modern history dating back to [Dwight Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower).[^cite_note-804] Until April 23, according to figures compiled by [FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight), Biden's approval rating stood at 42.3 percent, a slight improvement from the sub-40 percent level reached in the summer of 2022, but still well below the peak of 53 percent when he began his presidency in January 2021.[^cite_note-805]

An October 30, 2023, poll by the [Arab American Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American_Institute), support for Biden among [Arab Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Americans) dropped from 59% in 2020 to 17%.[^cite_note-806][^cite_note-807] The drop in support has been attributed to the administration's handling of the [Gaza war](74998519.md).[^cite_note-808][^cite_note-809]

According to [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.), in July 2024, Biden's approval rating dropped to 36%, the lowest of his presidency, prior to his decision to end his re-election campaign; his highest approval rating dated back to 57% in April 2021.[^cite_note-810][^cite_note-811]

## Media appearances

Biden was interviewed for several news outlets and appeared on several [late-night talk shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late-night_talk_show) throughout his presidency.

In December 2021, Biden appeared on *[The Tonight Show Starring Jimmy Fallon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tonight_Show_Starring_Jimmy_Fallon)* where they discussed "his [Build Back Better Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Plan), the importance of his bipartisan friendship with the late [Sen. Bob Dole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dole), rejecting extremism and getting Americans vaccinated against COVID-19."[^cite_note-812]

In June 2022, Biden appeared on *[Jimmy Kimmel Live!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Kimmel_Live!)* where he discussed "27 school shootings in America in 2022, why he believes nothing has been done so far about gun violence, an overwhelming amount of Americans supporting background checks, meeting with families after the [tragic events in Uvalde, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robb_Elementary_School_shooting), the idea of passing an Executive Order, the strides made in regard to Climate Change, [Joe Manchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Manchin) & [Kyrsten Sinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrsten_Sinema)'s voting record, housing, food and gas prices being very high and what he intends to do about inflation, the negative impact that the pandemic has had on families and the need for mental health care, being optimistic about this generation of young people, changes in the press, his process for flushing documents down the toilets, what his intentions are if Roe v Wade does in fact get overturned, and his hopes for America."[^cite_note-813]

In July 2022, comedian and host of [*The Late Late Show*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Late_Late_Show_with_James_Corden), [James Corden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Corden), visited the White House and spoke with Biden, Jen Psaki, and White House custodian staff. He also gave a fake [press briefing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_briefing) in the [James S. Brady Press Briefing Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_S._Brady_Press_Briefing_Room).[^cite_note-814]

Biden has also been interviewed by [Drew Barrymore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drew_Barrymore), [Jake Tapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Tapper), [Jonathan Capehart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Capehart), [Yonit Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yonit_Levi), [George Stephanopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stephanopoulos), [David Muir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Muir), [Scott Pelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Pelley), [Lester Holt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lester_Holt), and [Norah O'Donnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norah_O'Donnell), for *[The Drew Barrymore Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Drew_Barrymore_Show)*,[^cite_note-815] [CNN](62028.md),[^cite_note-816] [MSNBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MSNBC),[^cite_note-817] [Channel 12 in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_12_\(Israel\)),[^cite_note-818] [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-819][^cite_note-820] *[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes)*,[^cite_note-821] [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC),[^cite_note-822] and [CBS News](359551.md),[^cite_note-823] as well as a solo interview by [Heather Cox Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heather_Cox_Richardson)[^cite_note-824] and [Brian Tyler Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Tyler_Cohen).[^cite_note-825] Notably, Biden has not sat down with [Fox News](11121.md), despite its popularity in the United States.[^cite_note-826]

News outlets have criticized Biden for only doing a limited amount of interviews during his tenure. Biden participated in 23 interviews in his first 100 days, compared to 95 for Donald Trump (first term), 187 for Barack Obama, 60 for [George W. Bush](3414021.md), 64 for [Bill Clinton](3356.md), 70 for [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), and 78 for [Ronald Reagan](25433.md).[^cite_note-827]

[^cite_note-Schumer2021-762]: &#x20;After two runoff elections, of Democrats [Jon Ossoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Ossoff) and [Raphael Warnock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael_Warnock) in [Georgia](48830.md), there were 50 Republicans and 50 Democrats (including 2 independents who caucus with the Democrats) in the Senate. Both Ossoff (Georgia's class 2 seat) and Warnock (Georgia's class 3 seat) were seated on January 20, 2021. With Democratic vice president [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) casting tie-breaking votes, the Democrats also have held a majority in the Senate from January 20, 2021.

[^cite_note-Johnson2023-763]: &#x20;Kevin McCarthy was [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Removal_of_Kevin_McCarthy_as_Speaker_of_the_House) as Speaker of the House on October 3, 2023. [Patrick McHenry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_McHenry) acted as [Speaker pro tempore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_pro_tempore_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives#Speaker_pro_tempore) from October 3, 2023, to October 25, 2023. Mike Johnson was [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2023_Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives_election) to replace McCarthy as Speaker of the House on October 25, 2023.

[^cite_note-preInaug-764]: &#x20;17 days of the 117th Congress (January 3, 2021 – January 19, 2021) took place under President Trump, with the Republicans also briefly have held a majority in the Senate until January 20, 2021, and 17 days of the 119th Congress (January 3, 2025 – January 19, 2025) took place during Biden's presidency.

[^cite_note-Congress-765]: &#x20;Democratic seats at the start of each session of Congress. Independents caucusing with the Democratic Party (Senators [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders), [Angus King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angus_King), [Joe Manchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Manchin), and [Kyrsten Sinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrsten_Sinema)) are counted as Democrats for the purposes of this table. Throughout Biden's presidency, there were a total of 100 Senate seats in 435 House seats, so a Democratic majority in the Senate required 50 seats (since Democratic vice president Kamala Harris could provide the tie-breaking vote), and a Democratic majority in the House required 218 seats (assuming no vacancies).
